--- EXTRA: How NOT TO Let Your Slides DWARF You during Online Events ---

Never, ever let your slides dwarf you.
The reason I'm talking about it is because when you are doing a regular presentation on a stage, of
course you can have slides.
It's not a big deal.
It's actually very helpful if you design them properly, if you make them very visual, sometimes you
put a really interesting, fascinating, mysterious picture, then that actually triggers a lot of imagination.
You can talk about certain topic, bam, you show a picture of a of a little puppy and people are wondering
why there is a puppy on a screen.
This person is talking about technology, but then you explain why they connect the dots and they're
like, Wow, this is so clever.
This is so cool, right?
So it's actually really nice to use slides, but when you are doing an online event, slides can sometimes
dwarf you literally.
A few days ago I was presenting at an online conference and I was the only person who didn't use slides
and I got some incredible feedback.
And you see, the reason was that people could fully see me, but other other individuals, the way
they presented was a bit different.
They turned on the slides and they kept the slides on.
And imagine when you look at the screen, you mentioned that the full screen was just a slide and then
you could see that person just here in this little corner.
Right.
And the problem is that when you are watching a presentation and you see this huge slide and then another
one and another one all the time, and you can barely see the speaker.
Well, how can the speaker appear charismatic?
How like how can the speaker be engaging if the only thing you see is a static slide?
So sometimes when you present with certain types of software, it can get really tricky because you
have to choose.
People either see you or they see the slides.
Sometimes you can do kind of 50, 50 can split the screen.
You just have to double check with the host, with the conference organizer, how it's going to work.
Sometimes you can split it equally and then it makes sense to have slides because people can see you,
they can see your gesticulation, they can feel your energy, and at the same time they can see the
slides.
But when you know, when you double check with them, they tell you, well, if you turn on the slide,
you barely visible, you have to make a decision.
Now, in some cases, it still makes sense to keep the slides on, especially if you are talking about
something extremely technical.
You want to explain some really complicated process.
Yes, you probably need slides, but if you are presenting about something that's not very technical,
maybe you are talking about big picture strategy or you are sharing your story.
Right, or sharing some type of ideas.
Well, you have to decide.
And in some cases, it actually makes sense not to use slides, or at least if you use slides to minimize
them.
But you have to remember to do that.
This is a tricky thing, right?
Because if you are not used to presenting and then you start your presentation, you may not even remember
to to minimize the slides.
Every time you show a slide, you have to minimize it, then maximize it, minimize it, can get tricky.
That's why sometimes I just prefer not to use slides.

I need to give them?
And the simple answer is, Well, you can, because your presentation is the most important thing.
Slides are usually crutches, really.
As I said, there are exceptions with extremely technical presentations, but for the most part slides
are crutches.

40, 50 minutes.

happen if I forget what I have to say?
At least when you have slides, if you get weirded out, if you don't know what to say, what people
typically do, they read this slide, you don't know what to say.

=== Slides and Visuals ===


--- When Presentation Should Become a Conversation? ---

Have you ever had a situation when you went to a meeting and it was a meeting just with one person,
Maybe it was you and your colleague and that one person who tried to offer you something, right.
And that person started presenting to you literally stood up and started delivering a presentation.
Well, today I would like to talk about X, Y, and Z, and this is why you should consider our product.
The benefits of our product are A, B, and C, Right?
And you feel baffled because it's just you and that person in the room, maybe one or two more people.
And the reason I'm making this video is because I want you to avoid this situation.
I want you to consider the fact that whenever you are given a presentation to just one, two, maybe
three people, it's not a presentation.
It always becomes a conversation.
Okay.
Now if you have ten people in the room, it's slightly different, right?
You may want to present, you may want to pitch, you may want to do it in a different way.
Still maintaining a conversational tone.
You want to involve people, right?
We don't have a lot of people.
You want to involve them.
But when you have just one, two, or maybe three people, you always want to turn it into conversation.
Otherwise it just feels weird.
It feels awkward.
It's kind of like and again, I'm I'm pretty sure we have situations like this and it's pretty bizarre
when you are in a small room and you have a presenter and there may be five people sitting there or
maybe eight people sitting there and that person still uses a microphone like, let's see, there's
a microphone with speakers.
And it's just bizarre.
It's like it's surreal.
It's almost like, What are you doing?
We are in a little room, Everyone can hear you.
Why are you using the microphone?
Are you trying to, like, take some branding photos?
What's going on here?
That's right.
And it happens whenever it happens.
I just I can't just help myself and I just laugh.
I laugh because it looks pretty funny.
So don't be that person.
Don't be that person.
Now, I apologize if I offend you now, you may be thinking, Well, this is too obvious.
Why are you even talking about it?
But all types of people are taking this course.
And if you don't have a lot of experience with presenting, with communicating to groups, you may not
be aware of it.
And that's completely fine.
So make sure that you don't fall into this pitfall.
And again, whenever you present to one, two, three, maybe even four people, rather than just presenting
in a typical manner, always try to turn it more into a conversation.

=== Body Language in Leadership: The Non-Verbal Signals of a Powerful Leader ===


--- Correct Breathing: Why it's CRUCIAL for Alleviating Anxiety ---

In this video, we'd like to talk to you about a special medicine that will help you to feel much better
before presenting, but actually in any other situation in life.
And that's
because breathing.
Breathing is a medicine.
I know it may sound a bit cheesy, but I did it on purpose because it really makes sense.
You know, often we try to look at pharmacological solutions to our issues, not realizing that the
real medicine is already within us.
And yes, breathing is the medicine.
Now, you may think well, but we all breathe all the time.
Respiratory muscles keep working every single minute of every single hour of every single day.
They never stop.
Right?
You have your autonomic system that just keeps making those muscles work.
So you keep breathing whether you know it or not.
But the reality is that we forgot how to breathe properly.
A lot of us just just don't breathe the way we should be breathing, especially in a stressful situation.
So I'm sure we had moments when you were presenting on a stage or you were maybe negotiating something
or perhaps having a really tough conversation with someone and you had this shallow breath, right?
Maybe hyperventilating a little bit and you could feel your heart beating faster and you couldn't.
It felt like you couldn't get enough oxygen in you.
And I feel like the problem is we often assume that things are the way they should be and we have no
control, but we absolutely have control over our breathing.
So rather than just leaving breathing to chance, I feel like we should take more control in terms of
how we breathe, because you can use the power of breathing to make yourself calmer, more relaxed before
you give a presentation, but also while you are on a stage, it's really powerful.
So a lot of us just engage in very shallow breathing.
But when you breathe, you essentially try to get more oxygen and get rid of carbon dioxide.
Is the exchange of gases, right?
You bring it all to in and you are getting rid of CO2.
So this exchange keeps happening all the time.
Now, when we don't engage in the breathing, when we when we just have this shallow breath, especially
when we are in a stressful situation, like, like getting on a stage presenting.
Now what happens is, you know, that exchange of O2 and CO two is not very efficient.
Right.
And often what happens is we either breathe way too fast in a shallow manner, like, right.
This often happens, especially when your heart is beating very fast, when you are stressed out and
you can feel lightheaded and you find it hard to focus.
And one of the things that often happens when you get a panic attack is that you forget to breathe.
Right?
It sounds weird, but but this is often what happens.
Or you don't forget it, but you almost feel like you are unable to breathe.
Now, for example, what happened to me in the past was, you know, you get a panic attack in a social
situation while you are speaking.
So you start speaking.
And as you create sentences, normally you would take a breath and you take it easy.
But because you get stressed out, you speak very fast and it feels awkward to take a breath.
So you just keep talking.
But when you keep talking without taking a breath, guess what?

--- The Truth About Anxiety ---

In this video, I would like to put extra emphasis on something that I already mentioned a little bit
in some of my other videos.
And it's this concept of anxiety versus excitement.
Now, when you really think about it, how do you feel when you feel anxious?
Do you feel your palms sweating, maybe your trembling a little bit?
You feel your heart beating faster, maybe you are breathing faster, right?
So so you feel all of those things.
You may feel like this buzzy feeling, this weird sound in your ears.
You may even feel your blood pulsating here in your veins.
Right.
So what is the difference between this and being excited?
Well, the difference from the physiological perspective is almost nonexistent.
You feel pretty much the same things.
Your heart is also beating faster.
You may be sweating, right?
You may be really fired up.
But the difference is that you expect a positive outcome.
When you are anxious, you assume that whatever it is that you are doing probably has a negative outcome.
But when you are excited you feel very similar, but you expect a positive outcome and that's the difference.
Therefore, whenever you feel anxious and obviously there are some there are some exceptions to this.
If you feel anxious about, you know, about something terrible that just happened, it's pretty much
impossible to get excited about it.
But when you feel anxious before getting on a stage or maybe before going on a date or meeting a group
of people or doing a job interview or doing a negotiation, whatever it is, when you feel anxious before
doing something that could actually bring you a positive result, well then you can change it into excitement.
You just have to switch the outcome in your head.
Now, obviously, it's always easier said than done, but you can do it.
It's within your control.
So before you get on a stage, when you feel anxiety filling your body, you can imagine that you are
excited.

--- Basic Activities You Need To Perform ---

So one of the most important things that you can do before any speech is to really dig in and to do
research about your audience.
You want to find out as much as possible about them.
Now, a lot of people missed the step because, first of all, they are not even aware that this is
a thing.
A lot of people don't realize the importance of this step and others, to be honest, they just can't
be bothered.
But it takes some effort, it takes some energy, and a lot of people just are not willing to put in
that work.
However, if you do it, you will reap the rewards because your presentation will be well calibrated.
So what do you want to do?
Well, definitely you want to reach out to the organizers of the presentation.
Now, of course, this will depend on the specific type of presentation that you are doing.
If you are presenting, for example, at a big conference, you definitely should reach out to the conference
organizer and simply ask, Hey, kind of give me the breakdown of who is in the audience.
Can you please tell me what are the biggest challenges of those people?
What are the demographics?
So essentially your job is to find out as much as possible about your audience.
Now you may retort, Well, but what if the conference organizer can't be bothered?
What if that person just can't be bothered to give you all this information?
But in reality, it's in their interest because the more you know about the audience, the better your
presentation will be, and the better your presentation will be, the better for the organizer.
And I actually recommend that you say to them, not in those words, but I recommend that you literally
say something in the lines of, Look, whenever I do my presentations, I really try to put as much
effort as possible because I want to find out about my audience so I can calibrate my presentation this
way.
I can truly connect with them.
I would love if you can tell me a bit more about the audience, because that's going to help me to make
the presentation better.
And ultimately this will lead to a better conference.
You can say something in those lines and if you position it like this, you will see that the organizers
will get out of their way to give you all the information you need.
So what about doing a smaller presentation?
What if you do a presentation maybe within your organization?
Now, if you have access to the members of your audience, it's really worth talking to them now.
It never ceases to amaze me how very few people are willing to do it, but it's actually very simple.
Just walk up to the people who will be a part of your audience and ask them.
Simply ask them, say, Listen, I'm doing the presentation about the topic X, Y and Z, and I want
to make it as valuable as possible for you.
So I'd like to find out a little bit more.
What are you struggling with?
What are your challenges?
What are you trying to gain from this presentation?
And just talk to a bunch of people gathered intelligence and then based on that, you can craft your
presentation.
There's one more thing you can do, especially if you are presenting for an external organization or
if you are presenting within your own company for a lot of different people.
And it's a questionnaire.
It's as simple as that.
You can design a set of questions to find out more about your audience's challenges and aspirations.
What do you want to get out of your presentation or workshop or consulting?
And then you can distribute it.
Now, if you are doing it for the external organization, you can simply tell the House, Listen, I
really want to do my best.
I want to give as much value as possible to your audience or to your your clients or to your employees
or whoever it is that you're presenting for.
And tell them, Look, in order to do it, I need to find out more about your audience.
Here is a questioner that I would love you to send out to all those people.
Please make it anonymous so I can find out more about their needs and craft my presentation based on
that.
So if you do all of those things, I can guarantee you that with all of that new intelligence, you
will be able to mold your presentation into something that's going to be extremely compelling.

--- Why & How To Create Your Audience AVATAR ---

One of the most important things you should do before your presentation is to define your audience.
Avatar Well, so what does that mean?

--- Clever Strategy to Understand & Engage Your Audience ---

So here's something that you can do at the beginning of your speech to further connect with your audience
and to find out about them.

lines of, Hey, before we get started, I would like to figure out the temperature, quote unquote,
in the room.
I'd like to figure out who is here, who are you?
And then you can ask the audience a couple of questions.
For example, hey, I'm wondering how many of you here are employees?
Raise your hand.
Okay, cool, cool.
Roughly 20%.
How many of you are business owners?
Okay, raise your hand.
Okay.
Awesome.
How many of you are in the transition period right now and you're not sure what you're going to do after?
And maybe that's one of the reasons why you are here.
Raise your hand and and then you always tell them what you see.
So what I'm doing here is I'm not just connecting with my audience, but also I'm finding out a little
bit more about that specific crowd.
And also by raising my hand the moment I tell them to raise their hands, I make it more likely that
they will listen to me and that they will follow what I'm telling them to do.
If you tell people, Hey, raise your hand, but you don't do anything yourself, people are not very
likely to listen to you.
But if you say, Hey, raise your hand, if people are more likely to raise their hands as well, and
by telling people what you see in the crowd, you make it relevant to them.
If you just say, Hey, raise your hand.
If you have a business and a bunch of people raise their hands and you say, Okay, cool, now raise
your hand.
If we don't tell them what you saw, none of this is relevant to them because they are curious, right?
How many people here have a business?

Thank you.
Right now, obviously, this is just an example and you may be doing a completely different presentation,

to do it.

they are, what they are up to, and tell them to raise their hands and then let the audience know what
is the structure of the audience.
And this way again, you connect with your audience, but also you get to know them a little bit better
and that will help you to calibrate the presentation even further as you go along.

--- The GOLDEN RULE to Keep In Mind ---

Welcome to the section about the structure of your presentation.
Our structure is really important.
As I mentioned before, it lays the foundation for everything to come.
Now, one golden rule I want to talk to you about right away is that your presentation is never just
about the information you convey.
Because when you really think about it, if it was just about the information, you could give people
your notes, you could give them the transcript of your presentation, they wouldn't have to come and
see you present.
So clearly presentations are about something more than that.
They are about the perspective shift, the mindset shift, implementing new ideas, right?
You want to make sure that when people leave your presentations, they come out in a way different.
That's what you want, whether it's gaining more inspiration or looking at things differently.
But that's what you want.
You don't want just information.
And this is why it is so important to always start with the end in mind.
So a lot of people make a mistake when they design the structure of their presentation.
A lot of people start from the beginning, right?
They just go from A to B to C to D.
They put a few things together.
They put a bunch of slides.
That's it.
Ready to go.
But it doesn't work like that.
You actually want to start at the end.
You want to first of all ask yourself, okay, based on my target audience and of course, you already
know your target audience because you watch the previous section.
We talked about defining your audience.
So we know who is in your audience, what are their pain points, what they need, what are their desires.
Once you have all of that, well, now you want to ask yourself, okay, what is the outcome of my presentation?
What do I want people to get out of it?
How do I want people to feel?
And that's an important thing.
How do I want people to feel after they see me present?
And by the way, you may be wondering, maybe you think, okay, but I'm about to deliver a 20 minute
technical presentation.
I mean, it's not a really a big deal.
Still, you want to ask yourself a question, how do I want people to feel after that presentation?
And based on all of that, based on the outcomes that that you want you want to you want to kind of
define what is the end result, what is the ideal and result.
And once you have that well, based on that, you can then reverse engineer and you can create the structure
of your presentation, which we will handle in a second.

--- Simple, yet Powerful Idea Generation Strategy ---

So the next step is very simple.
It's the idea, and I know it doesn't sound very sophisticated and frankly, it's not sophisticated.
It's actually meant to be very dirty.
Right.
You want to make a dirty draft.
The reason I'm saying this is because a lot of people, again, they fall into this trap of perfectionism.
They feel like, well, I have to make everything perfect.
So as they are designing the outline of their presentation, the structure is try to make it perfect.
And because of that, they never get started.
And then they procrastinate and they end up just putting random things together right before the deadline.
You don't want to be like that.
What you want to do is right now you want to do the idea dump, which means that you take a piece of
paper and you literally rides down every single piece of content that you could add into that presentation.
Anything that comes to mind, it could be a piece of knowledge.
It could be a story, it could be a personal story.
It could be a reference to something.
It could be perhaps some type of case study.
Maybe it's an exercise, an action step.
Maybe it's a question that you can ask your audience.
Right.
So what you want to do is you want to write everything that comes to mind.
You have your topic, you know your audience, you have your list of objectives.
Looking at this, you want to ask yourself, okay, what are all the things that I could talk about
in this presentation?
Just start writing everything down and you may have this in your dialogue that tells you, Well, wait
a second, you're writing down too many things like how will you put this together?
It's too many things.
It's all over the place.
Look at this stage, it doesn't matter because this step is about creating the clay, right?
You want to get a lot of clay, you want to put a lot of clay into this basket so that later on you
can take it and you kind of malt a really nice sculpture.
But he won't be able to work on your sculpture if you don't have enough.
Clay So that's why you want to you want to write down all the stories, all the action steps, all the
pieces of knowledge, anything and everything that comes to your mind.

And this idea and once you do that, you will realize, wow, actually, I have a lot to say on this
topic and this is it.
This is the step number two.

--- The “Post-It” Note Technique ---

All right.
So this next step is extremely important.
It's going to take a little bit of work, but it's going to make it so much easier for you to design
a compelling structure for your presentation.
This is something that I've been using for many, many years to create my videos for those courses,

I've been using it to design articles and of course to design my presentations.
It's really powerful and it has to do with Post-it notes, you know, those little pieces of paper you

You can stick them to your monitor or to your wall to remind yourself of different things.
Well, what I want you to do is I want you to purchase some of those pieces of paper.

They get stuck because they don't have the big picture of everything that they are trying to do.

down on one of them introduction the second one, the middle, and the last one, the ending.
Okay.

And what I want you to do is you will position them on the floor.
Intro Middle ending on a floor.

Lie down on a floor, right?

Put it in there.

--- Something Not To Forget About ---

Okay.

So one of the most common questions people ask me is, okay, Jimmy.

Should I let people know what's about to happen?
And I want to answer this question here because one of the most common questions that I get, typically
the answer is yes.
Now, obviously, it depends on a specific situation.
Every presentation is different, but in general, you definitely want to let people know at least a
little bit what's going to happen in your presentation.
Now, you don't necessarily want to tell them about your entire structure and go into all the details
in terms of what's going to happen.
But you want people to have some type of idea what to expect, right?
People don't necessarily like the unknown.
People like to be familiar with what to expect, at least to some extent.
So what I recommend you to do is to focus on the objectives of your presentation.
So right at the beginning, when you're introducing yourself, when you are connecting with your audience,
let people know what they will get out of this speech.
Now, again, it all depends on the type of speech you are making, but by the rule of thumb, I would
say that the longer the speech, the more comprehensive the speech, the more complex and technical
the speech, the more time you should spend letting people know what exactly will happen.
So, for example, if you are given just a five or seven minute talk to perhaps your team members to
motivate them.

and motivation for the week to come.

it's a it's a short speech, right?
It comes from the heart.
You probably don't have to tell them what's going to happen.
However, if you are given a one hour lecture or maybe you are presenting at a big conference and you
are doing a 45 minute keynote presentation, or maybe you are presenting in front of all of the people
from your company and and you are sharing what your team managed to accomplish over the last six months.
Now, if that's the case, if those presentations are longer and more comprehensive, then definitely
let people know what's going to happen now.

--- The Power of Storytelling ---

Let's talk about the power of storytelling.
Now, storytelling is one of the most important things you can do in any presentation.
Now, obviously, it also depends on the type of presentation.
If you are presenting, if you are doing a keynote, for example, at a conference and you want to speak
about business lessons and things like that, you may want to add several different stories.
If you are doing a very technical presentation in your company, for example, you may think, Well,
why should I even use stories?
It's technical, and I agree that stories are not that important in this type of presentation, but
I still encourage you to try to at least implement some storytelling because very often through the
power of stories, we can take something that's very complex, something that's really difficult to
understand, and we can make it simple for people to grasp.
And when you think about a storytelling has been around for centuries, not even centuries, for millennia,
right?
This is how we used to convey information and knowledge throughout centuries, from generation to generation.
It's the power of storytelling, right?
You see, you could share a bunch of facts with people, but they are not very likely to remember everything.
But if you use the power of story, right, if you if you try to portray certain lessons or certain
facts within a story, it's much easier for people to remember.
So you don't even have to write things down.
You can share a long, captivating story and people can pass it on and on and on.
So as humans, we are hardwired to like stories.
You know, whenever there's a story time, everyone gets excited.
Obviously, as long as the presenter is doing the right job sharing a story.
But I'm sure that you remember that as a kid, whenever there was the story time, you got really excited.
You get really exhilarated.
You couldn't wait, right?
Because when people share stories, they take you on a ride.

You take a journey, and that's exactly what you want to do as a presenter.
You want to evoke emotions.
You want to you want to create stories that will captivate people, that will take people on a ride,
that will take people on this emotional roller coaster, because that's how you will truly engage people
and build that build that bond with them and make your presentations memorable.

Because ultimately what you want is people to leave the presentation room feeling like they've learned
something, but also being able to remember the things that you talked about.

want?

want to develop the core of the story.
You you want to introduce some type of a challenge, some type of an issue that has to be solved, right?

of that challenge.
And after the resolution, typically you want to add a lesson in there or some type of conclusion.
So essentially you want to introduce the story, you want to get into the core resolution of the challenge,
and then you want to finish with the conclusion.
So everyone knows exactly when the story began and when the story ended.

--- “What if I Don’t Have Stories?” ---

So a lot of people tell me, Jimmy, look, I agree with you.

You know, you may have your stories because you travel the world.
You do all of those different things.
But me, I don't have many stories that I could share on a stage, and I urge you to reconsider.
No matter who you are watching this video, we all have stories.
We all have stories.
The only problem is that we don't identify them for what they are.
But we all have stories in our lives, and sometimes the best stories are not very elaborate.
Sophisticated, right?
Sometimes the best stories are those simple stories.
But it's not about the raw material itself.
It's about how you convey it.
Right.
Something really simple could have happened to you, right?
Maybe you met a stranger and there's some weird synchronicity happening.
And it didn't happen in some exotic location, right?
There's no hero in that story.
There is no life saving event.
But it could be a compelling story.
It depends how you convey it, how you narrate it.
So what I want to do in this video is I want to encourage you to really dig deep and ask yourself,
okay, what stories could I share?
What are some of the stories that happen to me in my life?
And I'm sure that as you meet with your friends from time to time, you share different stories.
And I bet that you had moments, at least at some point, you had those moments when you shared some
type of story, whether it was 30 seconds or 20 minutes with a friend or two.
And they were laughing and they were fully engaged and they kept asking, Hey, so what happened next?
Tell me what happened next.
I bet you had those situations.
So the only thing you have to do is you have to recreate it when you got on a stage.

get on a stage, you have to have your stage persona and you become a completely different person.

person.

--- You Can Make 2+2 Equal 22 ---

As you are designing the content of your presentation, it's very important that you avoid the temptation
to just dump as much information as possible on your audience.
And I'm saying this because a lot of people, including myself, have this temptation to showcase our
knowledge, to showcase our experiences.
So so you have a 30 minute presentation.
It's very easy to fall into the trap of just trying to squeeze as much content as possible.
And I had those situations in the past when, for example, somebody would tell me, Hey, listen,
we want you to come over and to speak for our company and you will have 30 minutes, right?
And as I was designing my content, right, I had my structure.
I knew what my audience was about.
I would have that temptation of trying to put as much as possible into that presentation, because we
often think that the more the better, right?
So instead of doing two stories, you want to do five stories, right?
Instead of presenting just one case study, you want to do maybe six different case studies.
And it's the temptation that a lot of us have because we just want to do our best, right?
We want to give as much value as possible.
And we often equate value with, hey, do more, do better.
But it doesn't work like that.
Because if you try to add too much content into your presentation, the chances are you will be rushing
through it as you get in there in front of people, rather than taking it in and focusing on the right
body language and the right connection, the right energy that you create.
And also pausing from time to time, you feel like, Wow, I have so many things I have to talk about.
I just have to rush through it.
And it's one of the biggest mistakes that presenters make.
They just rush for everything, and it probably not is.
I'm sure you attended presentations like this where midway through the presentation the speaker says
something, Oh, oh wow.
I realize I have very little time.
Let me quickly go through this.
Let me skip this slide.
And you see that they are almost panicking because they realize that they completely miscalculated how
much time they need to present.
So what I want you to do as you are designing your content.
Sure.
At the beginning, as I told you before, you want to do your idea generation.
You want to you want to have as many things as possible in order, but then you want to select very
carefully and if in doubt, you want to have less done more.
Typically, as we are creating our presentations.
When you are practicing, I'm going to talk about it more in the practice section.
If it takes you, let's say 10 minutes to practice something, when you get on a stage, the chances
are it will take you 12 or 15 minutes, right?
Because when we practice, we typically speak faster.

We don't account for that.
So it's important that you consider this as you are designing your presentation.

22?

--- One of the Biggest Mistakes To Avoid ---

One of the most important rules of any presentation is that you shouldn't be using jargon.
Now, there are some exceptions to this rule.
Of course, if you are doing a very complicated presentation for an ultra specific audience, you may
have to use some really, really big words.
But but here's what I mean by jargon, right?
Obviously, sometimes you have to use those big words, but it's about the intention behind the use
of those words.
Now, if you are using complicated vocabulary of phrases, all types of terminology, because it actually
adds to your presentation, because it's something that you just have to use in order for people to
understand what you are talking about.
That makes perfect sense, right?
It makes sense.
You should definitely go for it.
But the problem is that a lot of us are using big words.
Not because we have to, not because it adds to our presentations in terms of the value, but because
using big words in a way, sometimes subconsciously, sometimes we are not even aware of it.
It just makes us feel important, right?
You may use that big vocabulary and you feel like, wow, I'm like this professor, I'm important.
I know those big words.
I'm using them in my presentation.
I feel important.
I feel good.
If that's the intention behind it, you should definitely avoid it because people can sense it myself.
You know, I've seen so many different speeches.
I've been working with so many different presenters.
I can sense pretty much right away when someone is using big words, not because that person wants to
give value to the audience, but because they simply want to show off.
They they want everyone to think, wow, look at that speaker.
That person is so amazing.
That person is so educated, right?
But people can read it.
People can read through it.
And you definitely don't want to create a situation where your audience looks at you and they are thinking,
well, yeah, the speaker is, you know, they seem full of themselves a little bit.
Right?
They're using this big vocabulary.
It's not necessary here.
And in fact, the more simple it is, the better.
And when you look at some of the biggest experts out there, some of the best speakers and professors,
what they have in common is that they don't overcomplicate things.
In fact, they can take extremely complicated concepts and they can transform them into something really
simple so a regular person can understand it.
So that's the power of true presenting where you can take something complex and make it simple for people.
This is the power, right?
So so if that person didn't attend your presentation, maybe they would need five or 10 hours to understand
that concept.
And because you are such an expert, because you can break it down so well, that person can understand
it.
And literally 1/20 of their time.
So remember about it.
I know that this is not relevant to everyone, but in case you have that temptation deep inside to use
big words, just skip it, keep it as simple as possible.
And this way you will be more captivating and the audience will resonate with you more.
And by the way, remember, the most important word you can use in any presentation is you, not I.
I've done this.
I've done that.
I'm amazing.
No, you.
Right.
This is how I want to help you.
This is what you can do, right?
You want to use you.
You always want to address the audience.
So do you enjoy this video?
Hope that you've been enjoying the views as well, and I'll see you in the next one.

--- The Single Best Way to Practice ---

So when you practice your presentation, one of the most important things you have to remember about
is that you have to practice out loud.
There is no going around it.
A lot of people have this misconception that it's simply enough to write things down and then read it
and just read your notes and then maybe change a few things and region notes again and then put together
your PowerPoint or keynote slides, go through them in your own head.
But the problem is that, you know, if if you practice just in your own head, you can't even hear
your voice.
You cannot truly feel the flow of the presentation.
And one of the reasons why people don't want to practice out loud is because they are afraid.
Right.
And it's weird.
It's it's counterintuitive.
Right.
Because why should you be afraid of your own voice while you're practicing without any people around?
After working with a lot of different speakers, I discovered that a lot of them have this pressure,
right?
That, you know, when I get on a stage I'll have to present is going to be extremely uncomfortable
then you know, I may as well leave that feeling for that situation.
Why would I want to feel it beforehand?
Right.
People don't want to put themselves into a situation where they feel uncomfortable.
But again, it's counterintuitive because if you don't practice, if you just practice in your hat,
which is not real practice, then when you get on that stage or when you appear in front of your team
members, you will not feel truly confident because you never uttered the words that are in your presentation.
So the only way to really develop this verbal fluidity, right, to be more flexible in your delivery,
to be more powerful, more charismatic.
Well, the only way is to practice out loud.
And it's so surprising that so many people, even leaders, even leaders who are doing extremely well,
they don't realize the importance of it.
So to give you an example, recently I went to Prague.
I spent quite a bit of time there because one of my projects is actually based there.
And as I walked into the office, I met this really cool lawyer.
So she's actually pretty famous within her industry.
She gives a lot of different presentations and it happened that on that day she was given a presentation,
so she was doing her thing in a corner.
So at some point I just walked up to her, introduce myself very briefly, and I was about to do my
own thing in a different office, in a different room.
And then one of the employees sitting there, he said, Oh wow, it's your lucky day because GB is actually
an expert in public speaking.
And you know, when she heard that, her eyes got really big and I just started smiling because I know
that look right.
Like she clearly, you know, she was in trouble.
She was about to deliver a presentation.
And then she found out that I'm an expert at this and I had some time.
So ask her, Hey, I'm just curious, so what are you going to be presenting about?
And we talked about it and she seemed a little bit stressed out, which is normal, right?
I mean, it's normal that before a big presentation you have a bit of stress.
But then ask her, how many times did you practice and how did you practice?
And she said, Well, I'm just writing things down and I'm just reading my notes and I'm reading my
slides.
And I said, Hold on a second.
Haven't you practice out loud at all?
Like, didn't you do any of that?
And she said, No, I didn't know that I meant to do it.
I figured it's it's okay if I just go for my slides.

--- The Solution to: “I wish I had more time!” Issue ---

I want to ask you a question.
So have you ever had a situation, especially back in the day when you were at school, when the teacher
gave you some type of presentation to deliver and you had a deadline?
Right.
You knew that the presentation will happen on Friday and that you prepped a little bit.
But obviously, as we all do, we procrastinated.
And then on the day of the presentation, right before, you just kept practicing and kept reviewing
everything.
And then the next thing you know, you go in front of everyone and you realize, Wow, if I only had
a few more hours, I would have done much better.
And if I only had two more days, I would have done extremely well.
Now I ask you if it ever happened to you at school, but sadly, those situations also happen in our
professional lives, even though, well, as the name says, we should be professional.
So if you have a presentation, we should actually prep for it well in advance.
But very often we don't do it right.
We have other things.
We are juggling various tasks.
We want to be in ten different places at once.
We all struggle with getting things done.
So we postpone, we push things away.
And often what happens is that whenever you are delivering a presentation, there's this voice at the
back of your head telling you, Ha, you should have done this earlier, right?
What if you just had one extra day, even three extra hours?
It would have made all the difference.
And I have to say, even nowadays, although it doesn't happen often, I do have those situations.
Sometimes when I'm presenting, for example, making those courses or getting on a stage and there's
that little voice in my head whispering, Well, it's going really well, but if you only had one extra
day, just one extra day, or even three extra hours of preparation or getting yourself into the right
state would have made some difference.
So for that reason, I encourage you to use a really interesting psychological trick and it's creating
a fake deadline may sound crazy, but just bear with me.
It really works.
So if you know that you have to give a presentation, let's say in five days from now, well, you will
be preparing as if you have five days until your presentation.
And the chances are again, we all do it that you will miscalculate and you will not have enough time
to properly go through everything, to practice enough time.
So you really feel like a winner when you get on that stage.
So rather than just sticking to that real deadline, what I want you to do is I want you to create a
fake deadline.
So in this example, if you have to deliver a presentation in five days, well, imagine that your presentation
is in three days.
Like let's imagine it not just that, put it in your calendar, put it in your calendar and brainwash
yourself that this is the reality.
Now you may retort, Well, Jimmy, this is crazy.
This may work for teenagers, but not for me, because obviously I know that I'm lying to myself.
But let's face it, we all lie to ourselves from time to time, right?
How many times you have to do something?
You know it's urgent, but then you lie to yourself that, well, it's actually not that urgent.
You don't have to do it right.
Or perhaps, you know, you have health issues and you shouldn't be drinking that Coca-Cola and eating
fries and that burger.
But, well, you lie to yourself and you say, well, you know what?
I went for this long walk so I can afford to eat it, right?
So as humans, well, we lie to ourselves as a defense mechanism, so why not lie to yourself a little
bit in order to improve yourself, to improve your performance?
So this really works.
I'm telling you, if you if you put enough effort, you can actually brainwash yourself that your presentation
is happening but earlier and then you can adjust your schedule as if that deadline is happening and
three days versus five days.
And if you do that well, you're going to put extra effort.
And then what's going to happen is once that day comes, you will realize, well, I'm actually prepared,
maybe not fully, but I'm prepared.
And that's when you kind of snap back realizing, wow, but hold on for a second.
I have two extra days.
Wow, I have two extra days.
And if somebody put a gun to my head and said, you have to deliver this presentation right now, I
would be able to do it.
Wow, that's incredible.
I would be able to do it and I still have two extra days.
Let's see what I can do with this extra time.
And that's how you take your presentations from really good to amazing.
Okay so use this technique is very simple.
May sound a bit funny, but it actually works.
I've been using it so many times.
I keep using it.
So hey, if it works then then you may as well try it yourself.

--- How To “TEST” Your Content ---

I really hope that you will enjoy this video because it's about practicing in a different way.
It's one of those things that most people never think about.
But there is one way to practice your presentations using your everyday situations, and this is how
you can also calibrate what works and what doesn't.
Now, most people think that when they practice, they simply have to take their notes and either read
them in their heads or just read them out loud.
But they never consider that you can practice different pieces of your presentation in everyday social
situations.
You can practice them with friends, with family members, and with strangers.
And no, I'm not saying that you you have to tell all of your friends to gather together, sit in front
of you, and then you deliver your presentation.
I'm not talking about that, but I'm talking about trying to tap into different bits and pieces that
will appear in your presentation and use them in various situations.
So for example, when you are talking to one of your family members, you can intertwine whatever you
are talking about with one of the points that you will be making in your presentation.
Or for example, you are talking to one of your friends and maybe there's a story that you will be sharing
on a stage.
Well, you can share that story with your friend.
You don't even have to say, by the way, I want to share with you something that I'm going to be sharing
on a stage because I want to practice.
You don't have to say that.
You can just say something in lines or, hey, you know, there's really interesting story that that
happened to me.
I would like to share it with you and then you share it the way you would share it on a stage.
If there is a case study that you plan to share or maybe there is some type of quote that you want to
talk about you in your presentation or an interesting fact or whatever it is.
Well, you can share it with people around you in real life.
And the good thing about it is that is very low stakes.
You don't have to tell them what you are doing, right.
You don't have to announce.
By the way, I'm practicing for my presentation now.
You are simply practicing by interacting with them.
But what's going to happen is, first of all, you'll become better at your delivery.
You will become more comfortable at delivering those separate pieces of content.
But secondly, you will get valuable feedback because for example, let's say that you want to deliver
a specific story in your presentation.
Well, if you share that story with a group of friends and you realize that no one is really tuning
in, no one is laughing, and maybe some of the people are kind of disengaged.
They are not paying full attention.
Maybe that's a red flag.
Maybe that tells you something about the story that you that you are sharing.
Maybe it means that you need to tweak something about that story.
Maybe it's the content, perhaps it's the flow, maybe it's the delivery.
But whatever it is, you know, the feedback, the nonverbal feedback of your friends tells you that
maybe that story is not ready for the presentation yet.
Maybe there is something you can do about it.
On the flip side, perhaps you're sharing something with a family member and that person is laughing
or they are fully engaged and they just keep nodding their heads and they keep asking you more and more
questions what that tells you that that piece of content is working.
On top of that, if you are sharing a particular piece of content with somebody, if they keep asking
you follow up questions, that may give you a glimpse into what your audience will be thinking about.
Right?
If you share something and that person asks you, Oh, but why did that happen?
Or What is the origin of this?
Or whatever?
The question is, that may give you an idea of some of the content pieces that you should add into that
particular content piece.
Right?
Maybe maybe you should expand it with something else to make.
The long story short, what I'm trying to say in this video and you should definitely use it, is that
you don't just have to practice by yourself in front of a mirror or in front of your phone.
You can actually practice in everyday situations with people that you enjoy spending time with, and
in many cases, this type of practice will give you the most benefits.

So don't focus on the process when you're about to practice.
Don't focus on the fact that, well, I'm going to practice now, it's going to be a bit uncomfortable.
I'm going to have self-doubts.
I'm not sure if I can make it happen right.

It wasn't that good.

--- Feeling overwhelmed? Here's how to handle it. ---

Let's talk a little bit about the power of Chunk.
So do you remember when you went to high school or university?
And right at the beginning you got a list of books you had to read and remember that sometimes it just
felt overwhelming.
You see 50, 20, 30, 40 different books and you're just pondering how the hell will I be able to read
all of those?
It's impossible.
But then of course, you go through the academic year and you realize, well, you have to read one
book at a time.
And then as you're reading that book, you read one chapter at a time and one page at a time, and once
you chunk it, you realize it's actually not that difficult.
Once you spread it in time, there's not that big of a deal.
So when people practice and it doesn't just apply to practice, it applies to anything that has to do
with designing your presentation.
You know, people often think, Well, I have to do everything in one go.
People think, Well, when I practice, I have to practice the entire thing, but actually disagree.
I feel like what you have to do is, first of all, you want to divide your presentation into different
pieces.
What you have the intro, you have the middle, you have the end.
You may want to even divide the middle into the main points, maybe divided into three different subsections.
And what you want to do is you want to practice every single bit of your speech separately.
So the way I like to do it, and this has been working so well and I've been teaching this to many different
people, is that let's say you take your intro, right?
Once you practice your intro, you forget about everything else, nothing else matters.
You're just focusing on your intro.
And let's say that you want your introduction to be roughly to say 5 minutes, right?
This is just an example.
Let's say it's 5 minutes.
Well, you can take your notes and you can divide that intro into a couple of bullet points.
So when you practice now, you don't have to think about everything else.
You don't have to worry that, whoa, what if I don't remember what's in the middle?
What if I don't remember that story at the end?
It doesn't matter.
Take that piece of paper, go through your notes, go for the bullet points and start practicing the
introduction.
Do it a couple of times.
Once you feel fairly comfortable, cool.
Now you move into the middle or let's say the first section of the middle and you do exactly the same
thing.
You take your notes, you take the bullets, you practice it out loud, do it a couple of times.
You move on to the next section and then to the next section, and then you do exactly the same thing
with the ending.
You take the paper, you look at your bullet points, you practice that a couple of times until you
feel comfortable, and only once you have all of that done.
Now you can try to practice the entire presentation from the beginning till the end, but use the power
of chunking.
There's no point of getting overwhelmed with the entire presentation.
You know, if you start early enough, you have the time and you'll be much better off.
If you divide your presentation into little chunks, practice them separately, and then put them together.

If you just speak from your throat, you won't be that powerful.
So some speakers find it difficult to have enough volume, especially if they don't have a microphone.
If you're on a big room without a mic, you have to speak up right now.
If you are using a microphone and you tend to speak aloud in your regular life, you have to be also
careful.
And that's why it's so important to test the microphone whenever you can, just to kind of gauge the
volume.
Now, recently I was at a conference where they had issues with the volume levels.
And, you know, I noticed that sometimes the speakers were very loud, sometimes they were not loud
enough.
It was one of those weird stages where, you know, it wasn't like this.
So so you didn't have a stage with audience distributed like this in front of you, but it was very
long and very narrow and the way speakers were distributed was just very strange because, you know,
at the back you could barely hear the speaker.
But then in different it was very loud.
So it took a lot of skill to calibrate that situation because if you speak too loud, well, people
at the back will hear you.
People are different.
Well, they'll have to cover their ears because it will be too loud.
If you don't speak loud enough, people in the front will be comfortable still, but people at the back
won't hear you.
And I noticed that a few speakers didn't calibrate.
They didn't test the microphone, they didn't know about the importance of it.
And sometimes they were just shouting into the microphone.
At least that's how it sounded.
Right?
It was really unpleasant.
And that's why it's just so important that you really pay attention to it whenever you can try to test
the microphone so you are aware of the volume.
And honestly, if in doubt, just double check.
Ask people, Hey, is everything fine?
Let me know, can you hear me at the back?
Is it too loud?
Is it too quiet?
People let you know and that's going to help you a lot.

--- The “Uncomfortable” Pause Made Comfortable ---

Let's talk a little bit about the power of.
So a lot of people feel extremely uncomfortable pausing during their presentations.
We often have this feeling that whenever we make even a little pause, everyone is just staring at us
and everyone is wondering what is happening.
Are you having a panic attack?
Maybe you're losing your composure.
What?
What is happening?
Why are you pausing?
But the reality is that pauses are powerful, especially for your delivery, for the engagement.
You ever noticed that when you are sitting in the audience and the presenters speaking and then suddenly
they make a pause?
Everyone is tuning in, right?
Suddenly even people who are on their phones, people who are not paying attention, they start paying
attention because, hey, if there is a pause, that breaks the pattern.
So as a speaker, I really encourage you to make pauses consciously, right?
And one of the ways you can do it is whenever you want to put more emphasis on a very specific point,
you can pause right before.
So let's say that you are a leader in your company and you are doing a presentation and you want to
you want to give props to your employees and tell them what a great job they did selling, let's say,
200,000 units.
You can do it in two different ways.
You can say something on the lines of, hey, by the way, congratulations to everybody.
We sell 200,000 units over the last quarter.
Amazing job, everyone.
Right?
If you say it this way, you are not really putting much emphasis on 200,000.
Right.
And it can get lost in the entire message.
It doesn't sound very motivating.
It probably will not inspire people that much.
However, if you say something, the lines of, Hey everyone, I just want to see massive congratulations.
Because over the last quarter we sold 210,000 units.
200,000 units.
Unbelievable.
Great job, everyone.
Now this makes a difference, right?
There is more emotion in it.
And because of that pause right before the number, you put more emphasis on that number.
So now everyone is tuning in and now it sounds like a big deal.
Anyhow, short video.
But this is something that's very important and is one of those low hanging fruits.
You definitely should be using the power of pauses and at the same time, when you pause, you also
allow yourself to regain your composure.
So let's say that you've been presenting for the last 45 minutes and you feel a little bit exhausted
if you want to catch a breath, taking a little pause.
It's actually a great thing because again, it's going to break the pattern.
People will tune in again and it gives you an opportunity to take a deep breath, to recalibrate and
keep moving forward.
And again, as you are doing this, I know it feels uncomfortable initially because you may have this
feeling that everyone is suddenly thinking that something went wrong.
And the reason that happens is because of the perception of time, right?
Your pause may take only one or 2 seconds, but in your head you may have a perception that you paused
for five or 10 seconds.
So the perception we have of time as speakers is completely different.
So take this into account, and what I recommend you to do is I recommend you to practice pausing.
So as you are practicing the delivery of your presentation in front of your phone, I want you to consciously
try to pause from time to time, then watch the video and see the difference.

--- Revealing Your Human Side ---

When delivering a presentation and talking about yourself, I really encourage you to try to maintain
that healthy balance between showcasing your credibility factors, but also using some of that authentic
vulnerability that basically shows that you are just a human being, shows that you are humble.
And the reason is that is if you just focus on your accomplishments, if you just talk about your qualifications
and incredible experiences you've had.
There's always a risk that you may come across as a show off person, right?
We come across as somebody who is too full of themselves.
So what you want to do is you want to have that healthy dance in between credibility and authentic vulnerability.
Right now, I know it's not easy and it's not something that a lot of people are used to, but it's
really powerful when you can, on the one hand, showcase why you are an expert in your topic, what
you've managed to do within your field, but then attach a little bit of vulnerability to it, for example,
by using self-deprecating humor.
Now, of course, you don't want to overdo it.
A lot of people, you know, they go down that route and then they overdo it and they they joke a little
bit too much about themselves putting themselves down.
You definitely don't want to do that, because that also shows the lack of belief in yourself.
But you want to maintain this really healthy balance where on the one hand you are confident, you believe
in yourself, you know that you are meant to be there, you are meant to be on that stage.
You definitely have a lot, a lot of value to convey to your audience.
But at the same time, you don't take yourself seriously.
And when you look at some of the best speakers out there, well, this is exactly what they do.
Right?
As you're watching them, you feel like, wow, that person is definitely qualified to do what they
do.
They know a ton.
They've experienced a lot, but they don't take themselves too seriously.
Right.
And there's nothing more attractive than that.
A person who is on the top of their game, but at the same time they don't feel the need to shout from
the rooftops, Hey, look at me.
Here I am.
I am amazing, right?
You ever had a situation when you met somebody and they were just talking about themselves and they
were telling you about their accomplishments and which company they work for or how much money they've
made.
And it just leaves this this bad taste in your mouth.
But on the flip side, I'm sure you had situations when you met somebody and that person was super humble
and very nice and asking questions and being curious and interested in you.
And then later on you found out, wow, that person is extremely successful.
Now that makes a really positive impression.
So, so be that second person.
Now, having said that, of course, as you are presenting, you have to share some credibility factors.
You have to tell people why you are qualified to be there.
But again, maintain the balance.

--- Time Management During Your Speech ---

When I talk to aspiring presenters, it seems like one of the most challenging parts of delivery is
time management.
So how do you manage your time, whether you have 10 minutes or one hour to deliver your presentation?
And I agree that it can be really tricky sometimes, but here are some of my thoughts.
So when you are presenting on a big stage, typically there is a timer.
It all depends on a stage, but usually they have some type of timer in front of you.
The audience cannot see it, but you can see it.
And in some cases they actually have another screen where you can also see your slides in front of you.
So you don't have to look like this all the time.
You can actually just look in front of you and you see your slides.
Now, what I recommend you to do is, rather than assuming that you will have a timer in front of you,
always double check with the organizer, double check to be ready.
Because the last thing you want and I had those situations, you expect a timer because the stage is
massive, but then it turns out that they don't have a timer.
And now you start panicking.
Now when there is no timer during your presentation, what you can do is you can ask the organizer or
one of your friends or colleagues to basically prompt you whenever you pass a certain time in your presentation.
So for example, you can say, Hey, when I have 20 minutes left, it would be great if you can just
raise your notepad and make sure that I see it.
And if you could do this as well 5 minutes before the end, that would be really useful.
So I know when to wrap up my presentation, so that's another way to do it.
But having said that, the most important thing is your preparation, because that's why you have to
focus so much on your practice.
You know, typically people who struggle with time management during their presentations are those individuals
who just haven't practiced enough.
Now, once you work on your structure, you work on your content, you know the flow of your delivery
and you practice it multiple times.
You will be able to calibrate the time extremely well.
You know, nowadays when I get on a stage, tell me 20 minutes, one hour, 75 minutes, whatever it
is, and I can calibrate to the minute without even seeing the timer because I practiced so much right
now.
I know that you may be a beginner presenter and that's completely fine and I know it's going to take
some time for you to calibrate.
But still, if you do enough practice rounds with the timer is going to be much easier for you to manage
your time during any presentation.

Okay.

Do I seem charismatic?
Well, probably not.

So instead of embracing this posture, well, you want to open up, that's for sure.
You want to open up.
You always want to stand up straight.
When you're on a stage, you've got to get your shoulder blades together.
You want to look straight ahead and you want to always embrace the power of open position.
So first of all, you will feel better about yourself.
You will feel more energetic, you will feel more positive, you will feel more confident, more open.

Right?

Right.
They don't seem fully genuine.
They don't seem fully open.

hey, I come in peace.
I'm open.
I'm positive.

Okay?
I come in peace.

--- Biggest Mistakes to Avoid ---

Let's talk a little bit about some of the biggest mistakes you should avoid when you are presenting.
So the first one is that you should never turn your back at the audience, and this is something that
a lot of people do.
Unfortunately, I used to make that mistake myself, but the audience doesn't want to see your back.
So even as you are walking around the stage, you want to make sure that you always face the audience.
Don't have to always face them like this.
But you may want to stand like this.
You definitely don't want to turn your back to the audience.
This is a big no go.
Another mistake to avoid is obviously crossing your arms.
We talked about it a little bit in the video about the power of your open position versus the class
position.
But one thing that's a huge no go is touching your face.
Now, this can be tricky if you feel a bit itchy, right?
But at the same time, you should really avoid touching your face.
And the reason is that we humans associate someone touching their face with being dishonest, which
not being fully genuine.
Right.
You probably notice when you see somebody talking to you and they scratch their face or they scratch
their eye or they're maybe covered their mouth, it just doesn't feel like they are very genuine.
And that's why you should avoid it.
Now, you may say, Well, Jimmy, but I'm itchy, right?
Mosquito bite me.
I get it.
But people don't know it, right?
You don't have a disclaimer that says, Hey, I got bitten by a mosquito.
I have to touch my face.
People subconsciously may assume that you are not fully genuine and that's why really try to avoid touching
your face.
And by the way, this applies to many different situations in life.
Pretty much any social situation, whether it's interacting with with people going on a date, doing
a negotiation, doesn't matter what it is.
When you are interacting with people, you should never touch your face.
Now what do you do?
If you really have to scratch your face?
Well, then scratch it, but scratch it properly, right?

--- Extra Video: Digital Body Language ---

Let's talk about your digital body language now.
Your digital body language is extremely important and some of you may be thinking digital body language.
Is that even a thing?
Yes, it is.
And in reality, a lot of us unfortunately think that the moment we get behind our laptops, our body
language doesn't matter.
And of course, when you are in a real room with real people, they can see from all the different angles.
Your body language is a bit more important, but when you are in front of your laptop, when you are
presenting or you are attending some type of team meeting, or maybe you are negotiating something on
Zoom, your body language is also very important for many different reasons.
First of all, what you do with your body will impact how you feel on the inside.
So if you cross your arms, if you slouch, if you sit in some weird position, that's going to impact
how you feel and it may lower your level of energy, it may change how you feel about yourself.
You may feel less confident, for example, if you are slouching or if you are, you are sitting like
this in a big armchair.
So it is important that you work on cultivating that positive, confident, powerful body language just
so you feel better.
At the same time, even though people can't see you from all the different angles, they can still see
you.
They can still see your posture, they can see your eye contact, they can see your arms.
So it's really important that you maintain the right posture, that you maintain an open position.
Now, one important thing about digital body language is that you have to position your webcam in a
correct way.
A lot of people make a mistake of getting way too close to the camera.
If I were to get really close to you right now, you may feel like I'm invading your space.
You ever had a situation when you talk to somebody at a business gathering and this is the frame, you
literally see their face right in the lens.
It feels a bit invasive.
It feels like they are too close to you.
It's almost like they are invading your personal space.
And even though you are not with them physically in one location, it still feels a little bit strange.
It feels like someone is invading your personal space.
On the other hand, if somebody is too far, it feels like they are distant.
They are a little bit defensive.
They are not even here.
Okay, so how do you position yourself in front of the camera?
Well, you want to make sure that half of your body is visible, ideally from the waist up.
You want to be visible on the webcam.
And the reason is that you want people to see your arms.
You want people to see how you gesticulate.
You want them to see your open position.
And most importantly, you want people to see your palms.
When they see your palms, they know that you are trustworthy.
Because remember back in the day when we were very tribal creatures, if you came up to somebody with
your hands behind your back, well, maybe you have a weapon there.
Maybe there's a knife, maybe there is a spear, maybe there is something else.
But the moment you show your palms, you're essentially, stop communicating.
Hey, I don't want to have any trouble.
I come in peace.
I don't have any weapons on me.
So you want to make sure that you show people your palms and you'll notice the moment you start doing
calls like this online, people will treat you differently.
You will come across as a more trustworthy, confident and charismatic person.
Also, another thing, eye contact, very important.
Now, you notice that in this course, sometimes I use my sunglasses.
Well, the reason I do it is because we are the Canary Islands.
The sun is really strong and I have a choice to make, right?
I either film in a boring room or I come out here to some of the nicest locations in the Canary Islands.
But when the sun is shining right into my, my, my eyes, obviously I'm squinting, right?
So sometimes I have to use my sunglasses.
But in a regular situation, you want to make sure that you never use sunglasses.
You want to make sure that you look people directly into their eyes.
Now, obviously, when you are talking to somebody via webcam, you can look at their eyes.
But if you look at the webcam, rather than just looking at the screen, you will create a perception
that you are having a direct eye contact with whoever is watching you, and that's going to make all
the difference.
So here it is, just some tips for making the most of your online gatherings and online presentations
to make sure that your body language is as positive, as confident, as charismatic as it can possibly
be.
