--- Introduction ---

Welcome to this section about the best practices after your presentation.
And it's one of those fairly short sections, at least in comparison to other sections in this course.
But the reason I'm putting it in here is because a lot of people have a misconception that presenting
is just about getting in front of an audience presenting.
And that's it.
That's it, right?
You finish your presentation and you can just go back home and relax.
And of course you could do that.
Technically, nothing terrible will happen if you do just that.
But in reality, there are certain opportunities that show up after you present and you should definitely
take advantage of them.
There are certain things that you should definitely remember about in terms of what you should do or
shouldn't do after your presentation.
So this section will be all about that.
It will be fairly short, but is very important as well.
So let's get started.

--- Taking Advantage of “Stage Effect” ---

Let's talk about something called the stage effect.
So let me ask you, have you ever had a situation when you were presenting and before your presentation,
you felt like a pretty unknown person?
Nobody really went out of their way to talk to you.
You were just minding your own business.
And then you get on a stage and then as you finish your presentation, suddenly people look at you differently,
right?
So people come up to you.
Maybe they want to ask you for a picture.
Or if it's a business setting, maybe they want to talk to you.
They want to ask you questions.
It's almost like you went from unknown to almost like a micro celebrity.
Now, of course, this will depend on the type of presentation that you are delivering, but there is
something called the stage effect.
And naturally, as humans, whenever we see someone who presents in front of a crowd, we give them
higher status, right?
Because this is the embodiment of true leadership.
If you get out there in front of people, especially if it's a big audience or really important audience,
it basically communicates that you are the highest status person in the room.
After all, everyone is listening to you.
Everyone is tuning in.
If you didn't have anything to say, if you didn't have experience and knowledge, nobody would be listening
to you.
So once you once you get on a stage, people assume that you are the highest status person in the room.
And it's so fascinating.
And a lot of people don't take advantage of that stage effect.
And fortunately, many speakers finish their presentations.
And as their hearts are still beating fast, they decide, okay, that's it.
It wasn't as bad as I thought.
Let me go back home.
Let me relax and I get it.
I completely understand this approach, but I believe that you should use that stage effect to your
advantage, because when you really think about it, that's when some of the biggest opportunities will
come your way, whether it's within your company or externally.
Right.
And even if you present in your own company for ten or 20 or 30 people, there is still that stage effect
to some extent.
You've been a leader of for the last ten, 20, 30, 40 minutes, whatever, whatever it is that your
presentation lasted for.
So right now, after speaking up in front of all of those people, your valuation is the highest, right?
People perceive you as a leader, right?
You just finished delivering a presentation and this is when some of the best opportunities will come.
This is probably a good time to have some of those conversations that are not easy to have that maybe
require a bit more confidence, right?
Maybe you want to introduce an idea for a new project or perhaps you want to talk to the CEO of the
company or to your direct supervisor or whatever it is.
Right.
This is the time.
This is the time because you are in the zone you've been just presenting.
You are in your flow.
You are in this talkative state and you are perceived as a leader.
You have a lot of power now.
You should use it.
If you are speaking externally, perhaps you are presenting at a conference.
This is the time to embrace all types of opportunities because guess what?
People come up to you and they'll tell you, Hey, wow, thank you so much.
I really enjoyed your speech.
I especially like the point about X, Y and Z and people will be coming up to you and you will notice
if you stick around, if you stick around, if you open yourself up to those opportunities, people
will approach you or they will give you signals to approach them, and you should take advantage of
that.
That's how you can get your best clients.
That's how you can make your best partnerships, right?
Because now you have this aura, you have this special aura, this the sense of a person, of a leader
who just was on a stage.
Right.
And I know it may be hard to wrap your head around it if you've never experienced it.
It may be tricky to to figure out how it feels.
But let me tell you, from my experience, it's incredible how it works.
It's literally almost as if you have some special aura and the doors start opening up.
But you know what's crazy?
The crazy thing is that that stage effect doesn't last for a long time.
Okay, so you become a micro celebrity for an hour, maybe 2 hours, maybe even a day.
But you can be the same people the day after or a few days after, and you become just the regular person,
right?
So suddenly the opportunities that would have showed up right after your speech, well, they are not
there anymore.
All right.
So, you know, Jim Rohn used to say actually that opportunities are like a cup of tea.
A cup of tea stays warm only for a little while.
So you have to take advantage of opportunities when they show up.
And that's why, you know, you should, first of all, stick around after your presentation.
Just stick around, just just budget that time in your calendar so you can be there.
So this way you can allow people who may have opportunities to work with you so they can come to you
and talk to you.
And also, if there are certain things that you want to do, right, maybe you want to talk to a particular
person, maybe you have a specific idea, right?
And now's the time to act.
Now is the time to welcome opportunities and to chase them as.
Well, because you have all this energy, right?
You are in a talkative state.
You are a winner state.
You feel like a leader.
You are perceived as a leader.
You have that stage effect.
So make the most of it.

--- How To Keep Getting Better ---

So after your presentation, when people come up to you to tell you why they like your presentation,
what was so great about it?
I highly encourage you to also ask for constructive feedback.
And I'm saying this because a lot of people will not feel comfortable telling you what they didn't like
that much.
And, you know, it's good for our egos to hear from everyone.
Hey, this was an amazing presentation.
You did so well, but it doesn't really help you to get better.
So what I try to do myself and it's not easy, but I try to push myself to do it is I try to dig deep
and I try to ask people, okay, thank you.
I appreciate your compliment.
But in all honesty, if you had to pick one thing that could have been done better, or if you had to
pick one thing that maybe I did wrong that you feel like was a little bit off, what would it be?
And the way I frame it is I tell people, Look, I appreciate your feedback.
That's how I learn.
So you cannot possibly offend me right now.
Anything you tell me, I'll take that into account.
And if you have this authentic approach of authentically asking for feedback, not asking just to seem
polite and to seem humble, but genuinely wanting feedback, wow, you're going to learn so much and
make sure that whenever people give you feedback, stay open to it.
Because the last thing you want to do is to ask for feedback.
That person finally summons the courage to tell you what they think about your presentation.
For example, they tell you, Well, it was a great presentation, but I think sometimes you are speaking
a bit too fast.
You know, it was hard to understand.
Now, you may have a temptation to now retort by saying, Well, I was speaking fast because I just
wanted to give you as much value as possible.
So I did it for you.
Right.
But this is not an argument.
This is not a debate.
Right.
It's not about that.
When you ask for feedback, you have to accept it, right?
You say thank you.
You may not fully agree with the feedback, but it doesn't matter.
You ask them for their feedback, so take it on board.
And the chances are there is at least some truth in that feedback.
You know, in the past I had situations when I would ask people for feedback and they would give it
to me.
And it's not easy.
It's not easy to listen to feedback, especially when you did very well and everyone keeps coming up
to you.
Hey, can we take a picture?
Wow, amazing presentation.
I loved X, Y and Z.
And then you meet someone, right, who's maybe a bit more critical and you ask them, Hey, John,
I'm actually curious.
You know, I have a feeling you can give me some really constructive feedback.
What are some of the things you didn't like?
What do you think I could improve on?
Right.
And then that person starts listing various things.
Wow, it's hard a little bit, right?
It feels like mosquito's just all over you, right?
It's a weird feeling, but you have to lean into it, realizing that this is the only way to grow.
If everyone tells you that you are amazing, you don't even know what you have to work on, right?
So for example, I'm not a native English speaker, so in the past I used to make a lot of mistakes
with my pronunciation.
But sadly, most people would never correct me.

And then eventually, after many years, some people told me, well, it's actually computer lab, not
computer lab.

Probably I still make mistakes recently.

--- Presenting is a Team Sport ---

--- The Power of Acceptance ---

Let's talk a little bit about fear and the power of acceptance.
So one of the biggest mistakes people can make is that they feel afraid before presenting, and rather
than accepting the state of affairs, they try to push the fear away.
Now, when I say fear, I don't mean accept it and just roll with it.
What I'm saying is that, first of all, accept that this what's happening right now is totally normal.
Everybody feels a little bit stressed out before presenting.
And as you build up the self awareness, you look at yourself and ask yourself, okay, what do I feel
right now?
I feel my heart beating faster.
I feel that I'm sweating.
I have thousands of thoughts in my mind, right?
You are self aware you are here and now and you accept whatever it is that's happening now.
You can try to shift it.
But first of all, you have to in a way, forgive yourself feeling like this.
And the reason I'm talking about it is because many people fall into this negative pattern of thinking,
Oh my God, I can't believe this is happening to me.
I feel afraid, I feel stressed, and I don't want to feel like this.
And they get -- off that this is what's happening right now.
But guess what?
When you get -- off, when you get annoyed by the current situation, it's not going to get better.
It's going to get worse.
Right?
So rather than just crumbling under that pressure of feeling fear, take a deep breath.
Ask yourself, what do I feel right now?
How does it feel my body?
How does it feel my mind?
Okay, I'm.
I'm accepting this for what it is.
This is the reality of the situation.
I'm just a human.
I forgive myself for feeling like this.
And now you can recalibrate and you can work on essentially reframing how you see this situation.
But I believe that acceptance is very important because every time you kind of want to push the fear
and pretend it's not there, it's going to only get bigger and bigger and bigger.
So instead you have to acknowledge the fear, get familiar with it, accept it, and that's when you
can start smashing the fear.

--- One of the Biggest Misconceptions About Confidence ---

One of the most common questions people ask me is, So, Jimi, do I have to be extroverted in order
to be confident?
And the simple answer is, of course not.
Now, for some reason, we have that misconception that in order to be confident, you have to be loud,
you have to have this really, really big, powerful body language.
You have to enter the room and talk to everyone and just love interacting with people.
Be super loud all the time.
But this couldn't be further from the truth.
There's also something called calm confidence.
And I'm sure you've seen those people sitting there in the corner, super chilled, relaxed, and they
have this aura.
And when you talk to them, even though they may not talk a lot, they usually ask questions and they
don't share that much.
But he can sense that not nothing can really phase them out.
You could try to start conflict with them.
You could try to frighten them.
Somebody could drop a bunch of plates next to them and nothing is going to face them out.
They have their emotions under control and we can sense that they have this real belief in themselves.
Now, interestingly, a lot of people who are super loud, super out there, well, secretly, they
don't believe in themselves.
I don't want to generalize because there are many different situations, but I feel like it's important
to mention this because a lot of people overcompensate.
If you look in the mirror and you don't like what you see, you don't believe in yourself, you feel
a lot of anxiety.
You you don't feel like you are good enough, but you want to be good enough.
You want people to feel like you are confident.
You want to be accepted.
You crave that approval and validation.
Then it's actually quite normal for you to try to overcompensate if you know that you are not confident
deep inside, well, what's the easiest way to go about it?
You go out there to the social environment and you overdo it.
Rather than being quiet rather than just taking it easy, you are allowed.

They just cannot get better at it.
But as I always say, confidence is like sport.
The more you practise, the better you become.
And it doesn't matter if you are extroverted or introverted, you can become more confident.

--- Accepting Your Past ---

A lot of people find it difficult to accept their past, especially if you made some huge mistakes in
the past.
But when you really think about it, we've all made mistakes.
It's a part of being a human being.
And I know that sometimes it is difficult to re-examine your life and look back and do the inventory
of all of the things you've done or you haven't done, and focus on all the negatives and focus on the
fact that you have regrets and you screwed up and you did some bad things or you didn't do the things
that you were supposed to do.
But here is a mindset shift that I want to offer you, and I feel like it's going to really help you
not to live in regret.
So as I look at my past, I see a lot of situations that embarrass me.
Like literally when I think about some of the things that I said or I did, I just feel embarrassed
thinking about them.
But here's the reality.
If you look back at your past, let's say you look back at yourself one year, two years, three years
ago, and you don't feel embarrassed about anything.
It simply means that you haven't progressed and let it sink in.
If you're not embarrassed about what you've done a couple of years ago, it means you haven't been progressing
in your life.
It simply means that you are probably very similar to how you used to be.
Right?
If you don't feel embarrassed, if you don't feel shame, there was no progress, right?
And everyone makes mistakes.
So what I'm trying to say here is that being embarrassed and feeling shame and and feeling some type
of regret is actually a good sign because it shows you that you upgraded your thinking.
It shows you that you have a completely different paradigm way of looking at the world.
Now you can recognize the mistakes of the past.
You can recognize those regrets and failures, and thanks to it, you have the awareness.
So in the future you will not make the same regrets.
On the other hand, when you look at people who look at their past and they say Everything was amazing,
I'm so great, I'm the most amazing individual, never made any mistake, don't have any regrets.
Well, these are the people who really don't grow.
They look at themselves in the mirror.
They think they are perfect, but they are not.
No one is perfect.
Everyone makes mistakes.
But those people, they never give themselves any room for improvement.
They never give themselves that push to to take themselves to the next level.
So, again, if if you feel some type of regret, if you feel bad, if you feel that you failed yourself
in the past, reframe it.
And rather than allowing that negativity to govern your life, realize that it can be your power.
Realize that it's a part of your journey.
You've learned a lot.
Every single situation taught you something.
And I'm sure that Stoics always say you can reframe things.
You can either look at the negative or the positive things are not good or bad.
Inherently, they just are.
A lot of people say, Well, I missed my flight.
It's a bad thing.
Again, it's not bad, inherently not bad.
It can be good or bad, depending on the interpretation of that event.
Same thing goes for breaking your phone or not getting that job or your business failing, right?

--- Always Customize Your Speech ---

So this video is primarily for those of you who present a lot.
So if you tend to present every single week, maybe every two weeks, you go to different places and
you are speaking about the same topics.
You may be tempted to reuse your presentations.
You have your keynote presentation, you have your PowerPoint slides, and you may feel tempted to just
simply reuse everything, have a template, share the same stories, share the same points, and literally
repeat everything you did during other presentations.
Now, for the most part, this shouldn't be a big problem.
However, I would like to mention something that's really important to consider, and is the fact that
sometimes you will be speaking in front of the same people, right?
The same people may be a part of various audiences.
And also when your presentations are being recorded, they will end up out there in the cloud.
They may be on YouTube, they may be on different websites.
You may want to put some of those videos on your website.
And the last thing you want is for people to think that you just memorize the one speech that they keep
giving in many different places.
Again, it's tempting.
It makes it easy.
If you deliver the presentation many times, you know exactly how to do it, you know how to start,
know how to finish.
But rather than succumb to that temptation, I highly encourage you to always customize your presentations.
If you speak a lot and let's say that someone asks you to do exactly the same presentation on the same
topic, instead of succumbing to that temptation, customize it, find out about that specific audience,
change things around a little bit, make a custom introduction referring to the audience, building
that connection with them.
When you share a story, try to share it in a bit different way.
And I'm sharing this with you right now because I'm sure that you've seen a lot of maybe not a lot,
but I'm sure you've seen some people out there who create presentations and all of them look the same.
There are those gurus out there.
I'm not going to mention any names here, but you know who I'm talking about, who delivered the same
presentations over and over again.
And I've been to some speeches like this, and it's always a bit annoying when you can predict what
the person will say.
So you've got that big speaker, he or she is talking about something.
You already know this story, but not just that.
You know exactly how they will narrate the story.
It just makes it very predictable.
There is no excitement.
It it makes you feel like you're wasting your time because you already know what's going to happen.
So why would you even be in the room?
It also makes it feel like they are not very authentic because rather than speaking from heart and customising
whatever they are talking about to the audience, they're just repeating the same things that they always
say.
And I want to urge you not to be that person.
I know that for most of you this advice will not apply.
If you speak just once every couple of months, you probably won't have this issue.
But if you are one of those people who speak a lot or you plan to speak a lot in the future on the same
topic, I highly encourage you to always customize it.

--- Don't Talk About Yourself Too Much ---

One of the biggest mistakes I see people making is that they talk too much about themselves and their
presentations.
Now, you may be thinking, Well, Jimi, but I have to introduce myself.
I'm sharing my story.
I'm sharing my lessons, my experiences.
And yes, I get it and I do it myself.
Even in this course, I share various stories from my life, but it's all about the intention behind
what you are saying.
And a lot of people have the wrong intention.
I've attended presentations where the speaker was just talking too much about themselves.
So when I was 18 I did this, and then at 20 I did that.
Then I set up this company, then we got this much funding and now my company does this and this and
that.

How is this relevant to all the other people and the audience?
And very often the answer is nothing.
There is nothing, right?
It's just that person showing why they are so amazing.
But there is no value for the audience.
Now, what I learned over the years is that the golden rule is whenever you are talking about yourself,
you want to link it to the audience, right?
This is your audience, this is you.
You always want to build that connection.
The other day I was doing a presentation for the Udemy community, actually.
I was presenting for the Instructor Partner Program and a bunch of other people working for Udemy,
and I was talking about being a captivating, compelling, confident presenter, and I shared some personal

the beginning.

there will be lessons that are relevant to this topic.
I'm going to share with you those lessons in a second.

to their specific situations and this is powerful.
So when you talk about yourself, make sure that you do it with purpose.
Make sure that you don't just share your experiences and what you accomplish for the sake of it.
Nobody is there for you.

People don't attend your presentation for you, they attend your presentation for themselves.

--- Never Be Late ---

So this video will sound like a no brainer.
A lot of you may be thinking, okay, this is very simple.
Maybe it's too simple, but you would be surprised.
A lot of people forget about it and I used to forget about it myself as well.
So when you give a talk, when you give a workshop presentation or when you meet anybody for any important
reason could be negotiation, could be a job interview, whatever it is you want to make sure that you
arrive early.
Definitely don't want to be late and you don't want to be almost late.
So a lot of people, you know, give themselves a very small margin for error.
They arrive ten or 15 minutes before their presentation and they think, well, 10 minutes is enough.
But here's the thing.
When you know that you are on the way to the venue and you will have only 10 minutes before your presentation,
what do you realize?
It or not?
Subconsciously you are stressing out.
Part of you knows that if something goes wrong, if if there is a traffic jam, if something happens,
if maybe there was a last minute, the room change, you may have a problem.
You may not be able to get there on time and that causes extra stress.
The last thing you want when you go to a presentation is to have extra stress.
You are already stressed enough because you're going to be out there, you're going to be presenting.
And another thing is that and I mentioned this in other videos in this course is that whenever you can,
you want to make sure that you get familiar with the venue and with your audience.
You don't just want to arrive and suddenly jump into deep water or get in Mike and go after it.
There are some exceptions if you are Gary Vee or one of those characters who presented on thousands
of stages.
Maybe you can't do that right.
You arrive, you leave the car, you get in, get a mike, and you go after it and then you go to another
place.
The vast majority of people cannot do that.
What you want to do is you want to arrive early, you want to get familiar with the venue.
Ideally, you want to get close to the stage, even get on a stage before the event start.
So maybe in the break, right?
So you get familiar, how does it feel to be on a stage?
How does it feel to hold the microphone?
How does it feel to get all those lights in your eyes?
How does it feel to look at the entire crowd?
You want to get familiar with those types of feelings that you will get when you do those things.
And ideally, you want to talk to your audience, right?
I talked about it in one of the previous videos and another section, but you really want to get to
know your audience by doing that.
By talking to them, you'll make yourself feel more comfortable.
You will get in your talkative state, you know, you will feel more relaxed.
You feel like now you are in a crowd of friends, not just a crowd of strangers.
It makes all the difference.
So please never be late.
What I would say is, if you know that your presentation is at 2:00 pm, try to be there at least at
1:00 pm, be there an hour earlier.
Now, does it mean that you have to interact with people for that hour?
Of course not.
You can go in there, check everything, get on the stage, maybe test the microphone if it's possible,
talk to the organizer, talk to a few people, and then you can retreat either to the speakers room
or even to the restroom.
Right.
If if you can find a quiet corner, just go to the restroom, go to the cubicle cubicle, do some breathing
exercises, maybe do some gratitude.
Just relax a little bit by yourself.
You can do all of those things.
But one thing I can guarantee you is if you are late, you just increase the probability of something
going wrong.
Now, unfortunately, this happened to me in the past.
I made some really dumb decisions in my life and I remember one time I was presenting it was actually
at my value in Barcelona, this event organized by Mine Valley, and I remember that I was so relaxed,
I was so chilled, and I got to the venue.
I was maybe 5 minutes before my presentation and fortunately everything went well.
But I have to tell you, I wasn't considering this back then, but I realized afterwards, wow, if
there was a traffic jam, if something happened on the way, right, if for whatever reason I had some
logistical issue, those people would be waiting for me.
And then the awareness that I'm late for my own presentation and people are waiting for me, that would
definitely destroy my confidence, at least to some extent.
Right?
You show up, people are waiting for you.
You know that you are there for them, yet you are late.
It's not very good.
So obviously I learned my lesson.
And you know, nowadays whenever I have a speech, even if it's a virtual event, what I do is I check
the equipment, I triple check the WiFi.
I always make sure I have some backup system right.
Even if I have access to fiber optic connection, I want to have some 4G on standby in case something
happens.
I want to have all my chargers.
If I'm using my laptop, I'm going to still have my phone charger in case something happens to my laptop,
I can swiftly move into my phone.
I'm going to let everyone know in the flat or in the office.
Hey, please.
I'm in that room.
I'm doing a very important presentation.
Please don't disturb me.
I'm going to put my my phone on the flight mode.
You know what I'm saying here?
I'm basically doing my best to prevent any potential challenges from.
Happening.
And I have to say, it just makes me so much calmer and it adds to my confidence because I know that
I did my best to make sure that my presentation will be smooth.

--- How To Dramatically Improve Your Presentation Skills ---

One of the most common questions I get is, So, Jimmy, how can I improve my presentation?
What can I do to improve not just bit by bit, but to make those huge improvements?
So I let you go from here to here to there.
What can I do?
And what I'm going to share with you in this video will be powerful, is going to be powerful.
But also it may seem a bit daunting.
And the reason is that it's going to require some work and it will require you being completely honest
with yourself and looking back at your content.
So what do I mean by this?
Well, a lot of people, what they do is they give a presentation and then they move on.

--- What If Someone Asks You a Question You Don't Know The Answer To ---

So what if somebody asks you a question that you simply don't know the answer to?
Now, this is actually very simple from my point of view.
You should never try to pretend that you know the answer.
And this is one of the biggest mistakes that people make.
Even though they don't know the answer to the question, they will try to kind of be as their way through
the answer.
And people can see it.
People can sense it.
If somebody from the audience asks a question and you don't know the answer, but you're trying to come
up with some random answer, people will sense that you don't know what you are talking about, and
that's a problem for your credibility.
Because when people see that, even though you don't know what you are talking about, you are still
trying to pretend as if you know what you are talking about.
Again, you lose credibility.
You lose their trust.
And what I've learned from my experience is that it is actually very powerful to admit when you don't
know something or when you are not sure about something.
So when I had situations, when somebody asked me some elaborate question that I didn't have the answer
to, I would simply say, frankly, I'm not sure how to answer your question.
It's a tricky question.
There are different things we should take into account when approaching it.
And the moment you answered the question like this, you show to people that you are humble, right?
Just because you are on a stage doesn't mean that you have to know everything.
You don't perceive yourself to be the person who has answers to every single question.
You show your human side.
Now, one thing you could do is if you don't know the answer, you could say something like, Well,
frankly, I'm not sure how to answer your question is one of those tricky questions that I would need
to take more time to really ponder it.
But I'm curious, what do you think about it?
Right.
You could do that.
You could actually fire back and a genuine minor say, what do you what is your opinion on this?
Now, you could also say something in the lines of, again, thank you for your question.
I appreciate it.
It's a tricky question.
I'm not sure how to answer it right now.
I need to ponder about it a little bit more.
But you know what?
Let me get back to you on this question later on.
I need some time to think about it.
You could do that as well.
But again, don't try to pretend you know something that you don't know because it sends all the wrong
signals.

--- What If They Cut Down Your Presentation Time ---

Hey, Jimmy.
So what if they cut down my presentation time?
What should I do?
And this is a really interesting question because it actually does happen.
It happens from time to time, depending on a situation, on the environment.
There are situations when, for example, get 60 minutes to present or you get 40 minutes to present.
And then literally on the same day, or even worse, an hour before the presentation or 20 minutes before
the presentation, the host comes up to you and says, Listen, we are running short on time and could
you please shorten your presentation?
Can you do 40 minutes instead of 60 minutes?
Or in extreme cases they may even ask you, can you have your presentation right?
I hope this doesn't happen to you.
It's not a nice situation to be in, but there's a way to go around it and it all comes down to your
preparation, right?
So a lot of people make a mistake of creating either a full script for the entire presentation, trying
to memorize everything bit by bit, or go in the opposite direction and not preparing enough.
And these are two extremes, right?
You either wing it and we talked about winging it and why it's bad or you almost I don't want to say
overprepared, but you do it in the wrong way and you try to have a word by word script for everything
to come.
Right.
If you do those things, you don't have a lot of flexibility.
If someone shortens your presentation, you don't really know what to do.
Now, if you take into account all the strategies, all the tools, all the tips we talked about in
this course, you will be fine.
Because think about it, if you start with the end result in mind, if you reverse engineer the process
and you think about the objectives for your audience, of course, first of all, defining who is your
audience, then you do your brainstorming, then you create a really compelling outline.
And then based on that all time, you create different content pieces and you add various stories,
and then you practice everything not just by reading, but also out loud, actually practicing and doing
all of those things that we've done before in this course.
Well, you will know your presentation very well.
And when you know your presentation very well, you know roughly how long each part will take.
You know that this one particular story will take roughly 5 minutes.
And then to make a point based on that story, you need another 2 minutes.
You will know that your intro will roughly take 10 minutes, you will know those things, and that's
going to allow you to very quickly decide how you will shorten your presentation.

Maybe you can remove the entire story from the presentation.
Perhaps you can shorten the introduction.
Maybe initially you are planning to talk about yourself for 7 minutes.
Well, if you have less time now, we have to decide what is more important.
The points you're going to make in the body of your presentation or you fully introducing yourself.
You may realize that you know what?
People don't have to know my entire story.
Maybe let let me make it short.
Let me take those 7 minutes and turn them into one and tell people the most important things, the most
important credibility factors about yourself and perhaps share just one vulnerable, super short story
so people know that you are a human being, right?
There are different ways to go about it.
But again, it all comes down to your preparation.

my presentation is I like to break down my outline and I like to ask myself, okay, so what would I
do if suddenly the organizer cuts down my presentation time by 10 minutes?
What would I get rid of?

--- How To Introduce Yourself In a Speech ---

One of the most common questions that people ask me is Jimmy.
So how do I introduce myself in a speech or in a workshop?
You always say that I have to make it about my audience.
I shouldn't talk too much about myself, but how do I introduce myself?
And this question is very good, right?
It's a very important question to ask yourself, because, again, the last thing you want to do is

So let me tell you how I do it, how I approach the situation.
And and this has been working tremendously well.
So whenever I do my introduction, I like to, first of all, connect it to the audience or to the topic
of the presentation or both.
And I like to mix credibility with vulnerability, credibility with vulnerability.
And when I say vulnerability, I want to make it authentic.
So rather than just telling people, Hey, I'm amazing, I've done this and I've done that, and look,
I've done even this.
If I do it this way, I'm not going to connect with them.
I'm going to seem like a showoff, so I definitely want to avoid that.
So what I want to do instead is I want to give them some credibility factors.
I definitely have to let my audience know why they should listen to me.
Why am I qualified to talk about what I'm talking about?
But in order to maintain a healthy balance of credibility, but also the humble energy, I want to add
some some vulnerability on the other side.
Authentic vulnerability.
Right.
I want to show my human side.

I would live the life I live now, I wouldn't have believed.

past.

--- Camera is not Your Enemy. It’s your Friend ---

It's hardwired.
We are afraid of rejection.
We have this feeling that know because we are sitting in front of a camera because it's filming us.
It will be there forever.
Right.

control.

to be at our best, trying to be almost perfect.
And that causes performance anxiety.
Right.

You have to remind yourself that camera can be your best friend, not your enemy is just about your
mindset.

--- Important Considerations ---

So as we discussed in the previous videos, you definitely shouldn't use slides as that crutch where
you just put all of your info and then you keep reading from it, right?
Using it as the foundation for your entire presentation.
So what you want to do is because you want your slides to be impactful, you want to make sure that
you don't overload them.
And again, this is one of the biggest mistakes people make.
They just put too much information on those slides.
They put a few paragraphs, they put a lot of crazy diagrams that in order to really dissect them,
you would have to pause and look at them for 10 minutes.
And obviously you don't have that much time.
So people just kind of gloss over them without really understanding what's on a slide.
And that slide just helps the presenter to kind of narrate whatever they want to say.
But it doesn't really serve any any other purpose.
It doesn't add any value to the presentation.
So the basic rule is you want to make the slides impactful and to make them impactful, you want to
keep it simple.
You want to keep them short, right?
You want to use big fonts and very few words.
So ideally you want to put a phrase on it, you want to use some bullet points, but of course you don't
want to make too many bullet points.
You may use three maximum four bullet points, maybe five, but still you want to keep the font pretty
big so everyone can see right away what you are talking about.
Remember, when people are listening to your presentation, they are primarily focusing on your presentation.
You don't want to take that focus away from you.
And when you load your slides with too much information and rather than listening to you, people will
be tempted to try to read the slide.
And guess what?
While they're trying to dissect what's on it, they stop listening to you.
They stop losing the context of whatever it is that you are saying.

--- How Curiosity Mindset Will Make You More Diplomatic ---

I highly encourage you to embrace the curiosity mindset as a leader.
Don't make quick assumptions.
And the reason is that a lot of the times we make a mistake of making very quick assumptions.
So for instance, let's say that one of your subordinates is not performing very well over the last
few days.
Now it's very easy to take it the wrong way, to take it at face value and bash that person.
Right.
Say, hey, listen, John, what's happening?
Like you've been underperforming.
I can't believe you know what you are doing.
I'm going to fire you.
A lot of people overreact like this, right?
A lot of people take things at face value.
However, as a great leader, you want to embrace this mindset of curiosity.
You want to put yourself in the shoes of that person.
And before you say anything, before you overreact, you want to go deep within and ask yourself, okay,
what could I be missing?
What could be possibly happening in there?
You want to put yourself in the shoes of that person?
Is it possible that that person is just being lazy?
Sure, it is possible, But it's also possible that maybe that person lost their loved one recently
to some disease.
It is also possible that that person has gone through a terrible breakup.
Right.
Or some other mental health challenge.
So it's so easy for us oftentimes to make an assumption, right, to assume that everyone else is living
a perfect existence with no issues, no predicament.
It's easy for us to assume that everything should be always working like a Swiss watch, but the reality
is that we are all in a human business.
We are all human.
We all struggle with various things.
So rather than taking things at face value, we got to give people the benefit of a doubt.
We have to embrace this mindset of curiosity, and rather than overreacting and firing at that person,
it's important to take a little bit of time to go deeper and ask yourself, What could I be missing?
Right.
Let's get curious what could be possibly going on?
What are other options that I haven't considered?
And when you start thinking like this, you'll be way more likely to approach the situation diplomatically.
And instead of bashing that person verbally, you will find the power to actually approach that person
in a diplomatic manner and talk to them, and they will be able to sense that you are genuine.
And when you genuinely want to find out what is wrong, that's when people open up.
People find it very difficult to open up in front of someone who is trying to bash them, someone who
doesn't listen, right?
Someone who is not taking anything on board, someone who already made up their mind why that person
is in the wrong.
However, when that person that you are managing can see that you genuinely care, that you want to
find out what they are thinking.
You want to find out what is actually going on in their lives.
They are more likely to open up about whatever is happening.
And hey, guess what?
It is still possible that that person is just being lazy right now.
That person is making a big mistake.
It's still possible, but at least you gave them the benefit of a doubt and is one of the traits of
a great leader.
So please embrace curiosity.

--- The Importance of Detachment: Why & How ---

Have you ever had a situation when you were leading a team and you had that predicament, that challenge
that seemed overwhelming, insurmountable, Right.
You're right in the middle of it.
And you felt all those emotions building up and you couldn't handle it.
And there are too many moving bits and pieces.
And at some point you just felt paralyzed, right?
You didn't know what to do.
So many different options.
Everyone is looking at you.
You are the leader.
You have to make some decisions.
Things are moving very fast and suddenly you feel paralyzed.
You don't know who to do.
Look, we've all had those situations not just in leadership positions, but just in everyday life,
right?
This is this is actually the definition of life, right?
We all face predicaments.
So the question is, what do you do in those situations when you get stuck, when you know you have
to make the right call?
But the question is, what is the right call?
What should I do?
What decision should I make?
Which path should I embrace?
And this is especially important when you are leading a team.
Now, it's one thing when it's just you and you're responsible just for yourself, but it's a completely
different thing when you are leading a team of people, right?
When you are responsible for them and you know that the decisions you make will have certain consequences.
The question is how do you handle those situations?
And one word that comes to mind is detaching yourself from the immediate situation.
Right.
And a lot of people call it differently, right?
So I remember Ebon pegging back in the day used to say that you have to get some altitude.
He used to say that you have to essentially zoom out out of your life.
Imagine you are getting on a helicopter, right?
You go up and now you can see everything from a different perspective, right?
Some people say, well, you have to get out of your own box and you have to see what's outside of that
box.
A couple of Navy SEALs, Jocko Willink and Lave Babbin, they say that you have to detach yourself from
this situation.
There are different ways to to speak about it.
But the point is what is important in those situations is not to get stuck right in the middle of the
situation, but it's important to get a fresh perspective, to get some fresh air to go out of that
situation so you can analyze everything objectively.
Now, when you're in the middle of a predicament, some type of challenging situation that creates a
lot of emotions and you triggers you, it is very hard to detach yourself.
It's very hard to think clearly.
It's very hard to be objective.
Right?
You are subjective because you are right in the middle of the situation and you know, on a different
scale.
Let me ask you, in your regular life, have you ever had a situation when one of your friends had a
relationship problem?
But then again, when you talk to your friend about the situation, you were objective.
It was so easy for you to say, Hey, just forget about that person.
It's easy to just move on.
But for that person, it wasn't so easy.
But then maybe later on down the line you had a similar predicament and another friend came up to you
and said, Hey, listen, don't worry, just move on.
That person is not for you.
And you couldn't move on, right?
And you knew that the other person did something wrong and the other person has flawed character, right?
But you just can't move on because you feel all those emotions, because you feel all the attachment.
You hear that song and it reminds you of all the great times you had.
And you keep rationalizing that maybe things will get better.
So again, when we are in the middle of that situation, it's so difficult to be objective and this
is why it is important to cultivate that detachment muscle.
It's so important to force ourselves to zoom out, to get out of that box, to gain a fresh perspective,
to see the situation for what it really is.
So when you are leading other people, when you face certain predicament, when something really tricky
is happening and you feel stuck and you feel paralyzed and you don't know how to make the right call,
right?
Or maybe you feel tempted to make certain decision to rush the decision, but you're not sure if you
are doing the right thing because you feel overwhelmed instead of just, you know, getting deeper and
deeper and deeper into that spiral.
It's actually better to paradoxically slow down and then detach, get that fresh perspective, reassess
the situation, and then make your call.
And it's counterintuitive because when we are overwhelmed, we have this temptation to go quick, right?
Quickly, make a decision, do something, bam, bam, bam, we want to rush through things.
But the reality is that and you know, this is one of those sayings in the world of martial arts.
You know, whenever I was doing Tai boxing or jujitsu, they often say that, listen, when you are
going slow, you are being smooth.
And when you are being smooth, you are being more accurate, you are being more precise.
And guess what?
Precise and accurate is fast, right?
So in martial arts, they often tell you, listen, when you practice that head kick, don't try to
go full force and full speed right from the beginning.
First of all, learn the basics, learn the proper technique, learn how to throw it from the hip,
learn all of that.
And.
Once you learn it slowly, once you get the precision, once you are accurate, bam!
Now you can really practice it with full force and full speed.
And it's kind of like this with leadership.
Sometimes you've got to slow down.
Sometimes you have to zoom out, get a fresh perspective, and then you get the clarity and you realize,
Oh, wow, I was stuck in this situation.
I can't believe I can't believe I was stuck.
The the decision I have to make seems so obvious right now, but this clarity will only come to you
when you are willing to zoom out to get a fresh perspective.

--- Why & How To Become a Connector ---

Let's talk about becoming a connector.
So ideally, if you want to be a great leader, you have to become a connector.
Now, this also applies to those of you who don't have a leadership title, but you want to embrace
everyday leadership.
Becoming a connector is really powerful because it allows you to create win win situations for other
people.
Now, unfortunately, most of us by default, don't think about connecting other people.
We don't think about those win win situations.
But there is something powerful that happens when you consciously ask yourself every day, What types
of people could I connect with one another so they can create win win situations?
And I have to tell you, it's a beautiful feeling when you do that, when you consciously do that,
and then people tell you after a few months, after a year, hey, listen, thank you so much.
You you connected us and now we have a business together or hey, thank you.
You connected us, and now this person is one of my best friends, right?
So if you want to be a more powerful leader, it is important to adopt this mindset of trying to find
opportunities for other people and connecting them with one another.
And when you do it, it actually increases your status as a leader and increases your credibility.
It makes you more powerful as a leader, especially when you connect people who work for you, right
when you create opportunities for them.
So you may be wondering, Jimmy, I get it.
I agree with you.
It's important to become a connector.
How do I do it?
Well, let's start by talking about how not to do it, and maybe you can resonate with this.
I had plenty of situations when other people try to connect me with someone else, and they didn't really
do it in a very diplomatic or powerful way.
So for instance, somebody would randomly add me to some group chat on on WhatsApp and they would just
type in, Hey guys, you're both awesome.
You should get to know each other.
That's it, right?
No introduction, no mention of who that person is, who I am, why we should connect, why this is
even happening.
None of that.
No audio, no video, no one person connection, just a text.
You definitely don't want to do it right.
So how do we want to connect other people to one another?
Well, I feel like one of the most important things is that we want to make it easy for them to connect
with one another.
So whether it's in your office space or perhaps at a social event or maybe it's at some type of conference,
you want to make it very easy for those people to find a common ground.
Because when you really think about how humans develop instant connections, it is about finding a common
ground.
Whenever you meet someone and he has those basic questions like, Hey, so where do you come from?
Oh, what are you doing here?
Oh, what is your business about?
Hey, what passions do you have?
In reality, what we are doing is we are fishing for touch points.
We are fishing for connecting points that that we can essentially use to bond with another person.
So you say, hey, where do you come from?
Oh, I'm from New York.
Or really, I used to live in New York.
Right?
So now you managed to find a common ground or, Hey, what are you doing here?
Oh, you know, we came here because we love traveling or really, I love traveling as well.
Right?
So when you think about your interactions with other people, every time you connect with someone,
you essentially whenever you are asking questions, it is not that much about you being so curious about
the other person, but it's about both of you or all of you trying to fish for those connecting points.
So when you are a leader and when you connect other people with one another, you want to make it easy
for them.
In fact, ideally what you want to do is you want to literally do their job for them, right?
So instead of just introducing two people with one another and saying, Hey, you both are awesome,
you should talk, instead of saying that what you want to do is you want to help them to find that common
ground.
You may say something in the lines of, Hey, John, Missy, you should get to know each other.

of Company.

She's in the same industry.
And guess what?
She's gone to the Himalayas next year.
You both are awesome.
You should connect now when you do it this way, you see what's happening here.

You create a common ground for them.
It's so easy for them to start a bunch of conversations, almost too many things to talk about, like,
Hey, do we talk about Southeast Asia?
Do we talk about business?
Do we talk about the Himalayas?
Right.
And this is just a random example.
Literally off the top of my head.
I could give you hundreds of them right now.
The point is, you want to find the common ground for the people you introduce to one another.
And also what you want to do is you want to build them up.
Right.
So introducing people to one another is not the time for depreciating humor, right?

--- Do You Suffer From an Imposter Syndrome? ---

So before we delve into confidence, I have to ask you, do you suffer from imposter syndrome?
Do you sometimes have those moments when you feel like a phony, when you feel like a fake, when you
feel like a fraud?
When you feel like people will find you out?
Well, what I have to tell you is that a lot of leaders experience it, unfortunately.
And the reason is that, paradoxically, the more shelves you climb in your life, as Jim Rohn would
probably say, the more you know, but also the more you know how much you don't know.
So as you start your life, your world is very small, right?
You are in your little crib, you look around, you don't really know what you don't know.
And then as you progress through life, as you try new things, as you go to school, as you get your
first job, as you travel, your world expands.
Now you realize, well, it's no longer my crib, it's no longer my neighborhood.
And my town is the entire country.
It's the entire world is the entire universe.
Essentially, you start realizing how little you know, you learn more, but by learning more, you
realize how more there is to learn.
And on the flip side, people who don't know that much, well, they often have an illusion that they
know everything.
And I'm sure that you met those people.
You know, they they've never been to Brazil, but they are experts on Brazil.

Right.

out.

Right?
And by the way, this is called the dawning Kruger effect.
You know, the fact that people who know a lot oftentimes underestimate themselves and those who don't
know that much overestimate their ability.
Fascinating concept.

--- The Feedback Sandwich ---

Have you ever had a situation when somebody wanted to give you a piece of feedback and the way they
did it wasn't very diplomatic.
They came up to you and they said, Hey, listen, I got to tell you, you really messed up.
You really messed up.
You did this wrong and you did that wrong.
Yeah.
Can you please fix it?
How does it make you feel?
It makes you feel defensive, right?
You probably feel like, what does that person know?
Instead of thinking about that feedback and asking yourself how you can implement it, you're probably
asking yourself, okay, when did that person mess up?
You're probably trying to find situations when they messed up so you don't feel bad about yourself.
And so you can prove to them that you are not the only person making mistakes, right?
So when you give feedback in such a way, it's very easy for people to get defensive.
Now, on the flip side, I'm sure that you had situations when you received a different type of feedback
that was a bit more diplomatic when the other person, maybe your boss came up to you and said, Hey,
listen, you know, first of all, great job on Project X like you did a really good job.
I like the way you handled all the stakeholders.
Now, there is something I have to tell you, you know, and please don't take it the wrong way.
But there's something I have to tell you.
You know, the way you did that particular thing, it could have been done a little bit better.
And here is why.
Right.
And you know, that person gave you some reasons and some examples and some solutions and then finished
off by saying, but listen, this is not a big deal.
I'm sure you can fix it.
And once again, amazing job on project a right feels completely different.
Suddenly you are not defensive.
Suddenly it doesn't necessarily feel like a criticism.
It feels like just a friendly piece of advice, Hey, you're doing great, but you can improve.
Now, of course, there are exceptions.
If somebody really messes up, if someone is stealing money from the company's bank account or if someone,
you know, verbally abused the customer, that's a different story, right?
You don't always have to be diplomatic.
But I feel like in most situations when we give feedback, it is important to be diplomatic and to create
a feedback sandwich.
Essentially, when you start with something positive, then you give the constructive criticism and
then you end with something positive because this is the only way for people to actually open up and
to listen to your feedback.
Otherwise, if you just focus on the negative, people are not likely to listen.
They are more likely to shut themselves down and again, try to think about different moments when you
messed up, right?
So so they can respond.
They can have a verbal fight with you.
Right.
Or even if they don't verbalize some of the things that they are thinking about in their heads, they
are probably focusing on that rather than thinking about your feedback.
So use the criticism sandwich.
Always start with something positive.
Then tell them about that criticism.
Do it diplomatically and then finish off with something positive.
Now, if this doesn't work and that person keeps making the same mistake, of course you may have to
be more direct and you may have to skip on the positivity and you may have to get straight to the point.
But initially there is usually no need to be super critical.
If you want people to be receptive.

--- This Common Behaviour Screams Insecurity ---

Let's talk a little bit about overcompensating now.
This is one of the worst things that you can do as a leader.
And I'm sure you had situations when you met somebody and they were very quick to tell you about their
accomplishments.
Oh, I've done this and I've done that.
Oh, by the way, that situation reminds me of how I accomplished it and how I accomplished that.

Right.

--- How To Get People to Listen to You? ---

--- Preaching vs. Staying Conversational ---

When you address big groups of people and you talk to your team, be conversational.
Don't preach right.
What you don't want to do is you don't be standing there saying, Hey, you've got to do this and you've
got to do that.
And by the way, Jody, you have to do this and you team, you need to do that.
You don't want to do it this way.
You want to be as conversational as possible.
You want to be likable.
You want to you want to remember that when you are addressing groups of people, you're actually not
talking to a group, You are talking to specific people.
You just happen to be talking to them all at the same time.
So for instance, when I go to different conferences, I may have 2000 people in the audience, but
in my head I'm not perceiving them as this big mob, this massive crowd.
I'm not talking to a crowd.
Crowd is not a being.
It doesn't exist.
It's just a concept.
I'm talking to every single person in there, one at a time.
I just happen to be lucky enough to be able to speak to all of them right now at the same time.
Because when you think about whatever comes out of your mouth is not interpreted by the group, it is
interpreted by each person's brain.
So, yes, essentially you are talking to one person at a time.
And this is why if you have issues with trying to get yourself to speak to your team, just picture
talking to one person at a time rather than trying to make one of those big preachy presentations,
literally imagine that you are addressing one person at a time.
And by the way, as you are doing it, literally give eye contact to everyone in the room.
And this in itself will help you to make it happen.
Right.
Rather than and I know, by the way, that this is easier said than done when you're in front of a lot
of people.
It may be very tempting to look far away, to look above their heads, to avoid eye contact because
we are scared while we are stressed out.
So it's so easy to look someone at the wall and just say whatever we have to say really quickly and
try to preach.
Try to avoid that.
Ideally, imagine you are talking to one person at a time.
The only difference is that all of those people happen to be in the same room all at the same time,
which is awesome for you because this is like the embodiment of productivity.
You don't have to speak to them one at a time.
You get to speak to them all at once and save yourself and all of them.
A lot of time now when you have this mindset, it will help you to calibrate your message and it's going
to help you to be a little bit more conversational.
Now you notice that when I make those videos, I tried these, I tried.
I know I can always do a better job, right?
There's always room for improvement, but I do my best to make it very conversational.
I literally try to imagine that I'm talking to one of my business friends and I'm trying to help that
person.
I use the word up a lot, right?
I don't try to preach.
I don't try to speak in a textbook language, but I try to make it about you.
I try to make it practical.
And so far, the feedback is this is one of the reasons why a lot of people watch my courses.
Now, again, I can improve and I try to improve every single day, but from my experience, I notice
that the more conversational I try to be, the better the content is.
And the same thing applies to addressing any group of people.

--- Don’t Make This Common Eye Contact Mistake ---

Let me ask you a question.
Have you ever attended a presentation, whether it was a big one or perhaps something a bit more informal
within your team and the person who was presenting never, ever gave you the eye contact or perhaps
you were at some type of conference or you were doing a group discussion with only three or four people.
And again, the person that was primarily speaking never acknowledged.
You never looked at you.
Now, how did it make you feel?
And we all had those situations, right?
It makes you feel like you don't belong there.
It makes you feel like that person doesn't really care about you.
We have this feeling that you shouldn't even be there.
You feel rejected.
You feel like, Hey, does that person even like me?
Right?
It's almost like they want to tell you something.
They want to tell you, Hey, you shouldn't even be here.
You don't deserve to be here.
And unfortunately, as leaders, oftentimes we make our people feel like this when we are presenting,
but also when we are just addressing a group.
So it is important to maintain eye contact with everyone involved, whether you are giving a presentation
or whether you are addressing your team.
Now, I'm aware that sometimes it can be really tricky, especially when you are talking to 20 people
and you feel already a little bit stressed out because perhaps you don't like public speaking, But
it is important that you do your best to acknowledge every person in the room.
Now, by the rule of thumb, the less people you are talking to, the more important it is to give direct
eye contact to every single person.
So say if I'm talking to you and there are three other people around, it is important that even though
I may primarily be talking to you, I want to give some direct eye contact to everyone else involved.
Right.
I may still give most of the eye contact to you since I'm primarily addressing you, but if those people
are around, I still want to give them at least some direct eye contact.
So what happens when you are talking to a bigger group, say, 25, 30, perhaps 40 different people
in your organization?
Well, you don't necessarily have to just switch from person to person all the time can be quite exhausting,
but you want to make sure that you give everyone some type of eye contact at least once throughout your
presentation.
It is important because otherwise they will feel left out.
Now, when the group is even bigger, say it's 100 people or perhaps 200 people even more, even at
1000 people at some conference, rather than trying to give eye contact to a specific person one at
a time, you can basically divide the room into different sections.
So for instance, when I present, if I have 1000 people in the audience, I may divide the room into
five, six, seven, eight different sections, and I make sure that I give my eye contact to each one
of those sections.
Because the great thing about eye contact is that the further away people are from you, the more wiggle
room you have in terms of your eye contact, Right?
If you are standing directly in front of me and I'm looking away, you can see that I'm not looking
at you.
Right.
However, if you go further, I can look a little bit away and you will still have a perception that
I'm talking directly to you because, well, my general direction is I'm looking at you.
So essentially, the closer you are, the more accurate I have to be with my eye contact.
But the further you are, the more I can deviate and look a little bit to the side.
So so what it does is if, say, people in your audience are 20 meters away from you, you could look
into their general direction and those ten or 15 people will all have a perception that you are looking
directly at them.
But remember that when you are talking to smaller groups, when you are doing group discussions with
just a couple of people, five, six, seven, eight people, give eye contact, every single person.
And this way you will engage them and you will show them that they are welcomed in their.

--- Avoid this Body Language Trap at All Costs ---

Let me ask you a question.
Have you ever had a situation when you didn't feel at your best, you just didn't feel it for whatever

Well, it was probably reflected in your body language.

So it's a tricky thing, right?
Because if you already don't feel good, it is reflected in your body language, but then that body

So if you don't feel very confident, you're likely to look down, you're likely to slouch, you're

You may use some of the pacifying behaviors.

We are not feeling very confident today.

And you notice at some point, maybe you don't notice that you are slouching, going so low like you
want to disappear from that room.
This happens a lot.
So in those situations you have to snap out.
You have to recognize, you know what, I'm getting into hide mode.
I don't feel that good.

This is my body language.
Now.
It's not very positive.
Let me feel better here in order to positively impact my body language.

wait for yourself to feel better.

And by the way, you can use this for your employees as well.
